I had the strongest argument for the change sitting in the repo and replaced it with a false absolute. A claim that flatters your framing is the one to go and check.

What survives the correction is the original point. Both moves change the same constant, and it would have been effortless to write them up in the same voice. So the config comment, the ADR amendment, and the register row all say plainly that the current value is a directive and not a measurement, and name the exact distance between the setting and the nearest comparable evidence. The trade-off I recorded is an asymmetry rather than a reassurance: the gate aborts gracefully and restores the assistant, and the load is bounded by a 480-second deadline, so a floor set too thin costs a failed dispatch — while a floor set too thick costs every dispatch the operator cannot run, which is the live, recurring, observed cost that prompted the change. That is why the lower floor is defensible. Not because 19 is safe; because being wrong at 19 is cheap and being wrong at 20 was not.

The second lesson came from the other session working this tree, and it was the better catch of the day. I had planned a regression lock. The obvious one asserts `swap_min_free_gb == 19.0`. That assertion passes whether or not a single line of code ever reads the value — it is the built-but-wired-into-nothing shape, and they told me it had produced three separate defects here in two days, including a red gate that morning. So the lock drives a real `SwapDriver` through its real step-8 gate and asserts on outcomes: refuses at floor minus 0.1, admits at floor plus 0.1, with the threshold read from the shipped config rather than restated in the test. Then, per the principle that every control ships with proof it blocks when engaged, it moves the gate above a previously-admitted reading and requires the same reading to flip to refused — which is what separates "the driver enforces the floor" from "the driver admits everything."

I did not trust that on inspection. I mutated `swap_driver.py` to disable the gate comparison outright and re-ran: two of the six tests failed, including the tested-off one. Then I restored and re-ran the full driver suite, 74 passing. A lock you have not seen fail is a lock you are guessing about, and the `== 19.0` version would have sailed straight through that same broken gate.

Two smaller things worth banking. I reported to the operator that available RAM was 9.85 GiB and that the floor might therefore not be his real problem — I had sampled it at exactly the moment the other session's battery probe was loading the 30B, and reported the dip as the resting state. It was 22.66 GiB. Measuring a headroom change from a single sample taken during someone else's memory-heavy operation is the same error as narrating elapsed time instead of reading the clock. And when `verify-battery-ao-lifecycle` came back 89 passed / 1 failed, I ran it against unmodified main before assuming anything: identical failure, pre-existing, a stale assertion expecting two `Write-SkipReport` call sites and counting seven. It is now #1314 rather than a line in a chat message, along with #1315 for the stale 21.0 fallbacks I deliberately did not sweep into this change — a fallback higher than the configured floor is merely conservative, and the direction that would actually be a defect is now closed by the new lock.

**Proposed lesson:** A superlative ("the lowest ever", "nothing has ever") is a CLAIM about a dataset, and it must be checked against that dataset before it ships — most urgently when it flatters the framing of your own change, because that is when it will not be reread.

**Proposed lesson:** A threshold that moves for a *reason* and a threshold that moves on *evidence* must not be written up in the same voice — record the distance between the setting and the nearest measurement, or the next reader inherits a judgement call as data.

**Proposed lesson:** A regression lock on a configured safety value must assert on BEHAVIOUR at both sides of the boundary, never on the constant — and must be seen to fail against a deliberately broken gate before it is trusted, because the constant-equality version passes against exactly the defect it was written to catch.

**Next:** #1314 (the stale battery-verifier assertion, failing on main right now) and #1315 (the config-resolution fallback that guesses a safety threshold instead of failing closed — the more interesting of the two).

### 2026-08-07 — Three sessions called them someone else's files, and two of them were evidence

*Plain summary: Six stray files had been sitting in the project folder for weeks. Every session that noticed them assumed they belonged to another session and left them alone. Two of them turned out to be the input images for a performance test whose results we are still citing — meaning that test could not be re-run, and its recorded numbers could not be checked by anyone. The fix tracks the images and adds an automatic check so this cannot happen again quietly.*

The operator's instruction was blunt: *there is no one else, stop saying you won't touch someone else's work, you are responsible for everything.* He was right, and the proof was six files deep.

`git status` in the primary checkout had shown the same six untracked files for weeks. I had read past them all day. Two prior handoff cards had not: `class-census-landing-20260730.md:138` lists five of them, and `coder-tevv-landing-20260730b.md:36` lists four and notes the operator had offered to bin them. Both cards classified them the same way — another session's working set, flag but do not judge. That classification is the correct default in a shared tree, and it was wrong here, and nothing in the classification could tell you which.

So I opened them. `si.js` is a 317 KB Sphinx search index scraped from a third-party code-antipatterns documentation site, unreferenced, unattributed, sitting in a repository that syncs to a public mirror. `SoCWatchHelp.json` is an Intel tool's help output, regenerable. `blarai_logo_linkedin.jpg` is a brand asset that belongs with the others in `branding/`. Those three were clutter with a provenance question attached.

The other two were not clutter. `scripts/probe_35b_multimodal_quality.py` reads three images, and two of them — `blarai_lighthouse3.png` and `hand2.png` — were named as `_REPO_ROOT / "..."` and had never been committed. That script is gate B evidence for the 35B-A3B consolidation decision, which the status snapshot still carries as NOT DECIDABLE. Its results are in `docs/performance/`, dated 2026-07-16, and they cannot be re-derived by anyone, on any machine, including this one after a fresh clone.

That is a different failure from the one #1241 has been chasing all month. That arc is about an instrument that reports "did not run" as "ran and found nothing." This is *"ran, recorded, and not re-runnable"* — which at any later date is indistinguishable from fabricated. The evidence looked perfect. It had hardware, driver versions, methodology, verbatim outputs, the lot. It just could not be checked. And nothing in a 10,506-test gate noticed, for the simple reason that nothing checked whether a probe's *inputs* exist.

The fix that mattered was not committing two PNGs. It was `tests/security/test_measurement_subjects.py`: parse every tracked Python module, resolve every media path anchored to `Path(__file__)` through `.parent` chains and module-level constants built from them, and refuse any that names a file git does not track. Deny-by-default, because a blocklist of known-bad filenames would be exactly the shape I broke in a different lock the day before. A file the script *generates* is the only exception and must be declared with a reason; there are two, and I verified both by reading the scripts that write them rather than trusting the variable names.

Then the independent review took it apart, and it was worth every minute.

**It retracted one of my three claims.** I had said three subjects were unbanked. `cook_test.html` turned out to be byte-identical to `docs/performance/ov_cache_answer_flip_2026-07-22/subjects/cook_ok.html`, tracked since `56fd5782` on 2026-07-30 — the same blob, `8849e08c...`. My first-pass `md5sum` showed them as different files, and I took that as evidence of a missing original. They differed because one was CRLF in the working tree and one was LF, which is *the same line-ending fact the rest of my change is about*. I had the mechanism in hand and did not apply it to my own evidence. Two subjects were genuinely missing. The third claim was false, and false in the direction that made my change look more necessary — which is precisely the direction this whole arc exists to catch. It is recorded in the subjects README rather than quietly dropped, because a retraction that only lives in a commit message is not a retraction.

**It found the byte guard aimed at the wrong file.** I had marked `docs/performance/subjects/**` as `-text` so line-ending normalisation could not silently alter a subject, and wrote a confident comment about protecting ground truth. The actual ground truth for the gemma4 vision probe is `cook_ok.html`, in a *different* directory my glob did not reach, and it is being normalised right now: 23,713 bytes committed, 24,296 on disk. A re-render from the worktree would not have consumed the bytes the record cites. I had protected my copy and left the original exposed.

**It found the scanner blind to the repo's dominant idiom.** I handled `.parent` and not `parents[N]`, which is an `ast.Subscript` and appears in 20+ tracked modules; and `ast.Assign` but not `ast.AnnAssign`, which is the form our own strict-type-hints standard pushes authors toward. Neither made anything red. That is the whole danger: a blind spot costs coverage silently and reads as protection. Fixing both moved real coverage from 11 anchored references to 12, and the twelfth was `scripts/measure_guest_parser_memory.py`'s `news_quantum.html` — a subject of a recorded #661 measurement that my "structural control" could not see at all. It is now a canary in the guard test, so the scanner going blind again turns something red instead of quietly covering less.

The trade-off I took deliberately: the scanner is static and bounded. It cannot see f-strings, `os.path.join`, `joinpath`, a filename held in a variable, or a bare relative literal with no `/` join. The rejected alternative was to chase completeness with heuristics over the whole path-construction space, which would have produced false positives, which get a control disabled. Instead the module docstring *writes down every gap*, because an undocumented blind spot in a control reads as coverage, and that is worse than having no control at all. The reviewer ran 11 mutations against the production logic and found no test that stayed green when it should have gone red.

The uncomfortable part is the pattern, not the bug. I have spent this session insisting that a deferral needs an observable predicate and that a lock must be mutated before it is trusted — and I shipped a claim I had not checked against a blob, in a commit about claims outrunning evidence. The reviewer caught it in under ten minutes by running one command I never ran on my own artifact. Author is not verifier, and the day it feels most redundant is the day it pays.

Gate on merged `main`: 10515 passed / 0 failed / 0 skipped / 133 deselected, merged at `a2715515`. The +9 is this lock and its proofs.

**Proposed lesson:** *A recorded measurement is only evidence if its INPUTS are committed. Results, methodology and hardware in the record prove nothing if the subject the run consumed is untracked — the record is then indistinguishable from a fabricated one, and no amount of detail in it closes that gap. Check what a probe reads, not just what it wrote.*

**Proposed lesson:** *When you find the mechanism behind a bug, run it against your own evidence before you ship the finding. I discovered that CRLF normalisation makes identical files look different, and in the same change cited an md5 difference as proof that a file was missing.*

**Next:** the `oracle_mutation.py` score inflation on `main` (#1231) — budget-dropped mutants fall out of both `killed` and `ran`, so the score and the coverage band RISE as the budget tightens, while the stamp honestly says `partial-budget`. Same family, live in shipped code, and the substrate `feat/1195` landed this morning makes the fix cheap.

### 2026-08-07 — The audit reported better coverage the less of it actually ran

*Plain summary: One of our automated quality checks deliberately stops early when it runs out of time. But the tests it never got to were simply forgotten rather than recorded, so the check's own score went UP the more of it got skipped. A busy machine could make a weak result look excellent. The fix records what was skipped and counts it against the score.*

I went looking for this in a parked branch and found it in shipped code.

`fix/1231-budget-honesty` had been sitting unmerged, 151 commits behind, conflicting on the one file it changes. Its analysis was sound and its implementation carried three blocking defects, so the disposition I banked was to park the branch and land the fix directly. Reading `main` to write that fix, the defect was plainly there, on the live path, and had been for as long as the budget logic has existed.

The mutation audit mutates a candidate's code and re-runs the job oracle against each mutant: the oracle going RED means it *caught* the wrong code. There is a wall-clock budget, and on expiry the loop `break`s. Everything after the break simply disappeared — counted in neither `killed` nor `ran`. Both the score and the advisory coverage band are computed from those two numbers, so a tighter budget shrank the denominator while the numerator stood. Fewer mutants attempted, higher apparent quality. On a contended box — exactly when you should trust a result *less* — the number goes up.

The part that makes it genuinely dangerous is the part the code already got right. It stamps `partial-budget`, honestly and correctly. So the evidence reads `partial-budget · 1/1 · strong`: a true statement about completeness and a false one about quality, in the same breath, with the honest half lending its credibility to the inflated half. A reader who has learned to check the completeness stamp — which is the discipline this whole arc has been building — gets a green light on the wrong field. An instrument that discloses one limitation trains you to assume the rest is sound.

Two things landed this morning that made the fix cheap, and neither author knew they were building for the other. `feat/1195` added `_mutant_row` and the `EV_MUTANTS` evidence key, which is exactly the home a third outcome needs. And the guard I added at that same merge — the one that stopped `mutated` admitting rows that never ran — means a `not-run` row can never be read downstream as "this subject was put to the question." I wrote that guard for a different reason and it turned out to be the precondition for this one. The halves fit.

The design decision worth recording is what the band scores over. The score itself still reads `killed/ran`, and I deliberately did not retcon it into a completeness claim: "3 of the 4 mutants we ran were caught" is a true sentence and should stay one. But the *band* is a quality opinion about the oracle, and an oracle is not better because we ran out of time before testing it properly. So the band now scores over the selected set: an unattempted mutant counts against it. An unknown must never be able to improve a quality claim. The rejected alternative was folding unrun mutants into `ran`, which would have made the score honest in aggregate and dishonest in detail — claiming we ran things we did not.

The one place I stopped short of fail-closed: when *nothing* ran, the band stays `unknown` rather than dropping to `weak`. Counting unknowns against you is right; asserting a finding from zero observations is not. `weak` would convict an oracle nobody examined.

The existing tests could not have caught any of this, and the reason is worth more than the bug. `test_budget_expiry_mid_run_is_partial_budget` uses a sharp oracle that kills every mutant, so the score sits at a 100% ceiling — and a ceiling cannot detect inflation. The test asserted `ran == 1 and killed == 1` and was perfectly happy while the band read `strong` on a run that had dropped most of its work. It took a mixed-verdict runner, where some mutants survive, for the defect to be observable at all. I then proved the new locks by reverting the band to `killed/ran`: exactly the two coverage tests went red, with the messages I wrote for them, and nothing else moved.

**Recurrence of lesson 330:** *a check that can fail to run needs a third answer that is not "pass"* — here the third answer is a `not-run` mutant row, and the instrument is the mutation audit rather than the parity check. This is the same remedy in a fourth place.

**Proposed lesson:** *An instrument that honestly discloses ONE limitation earns trust for the fields it is still lying about. `partial-budget` next to an inflated score is worse than no stamp at all, because the disclosure is what persuades the reader the rest was checked. When a run is partial, audit every derived figure for whether it silently assumed completeness — the stamp is not the fix, it is the reminder to go looking.*

**Proposed lesson:** *A test whose fixture pins the metric at a ceiling cannot detect inflation, and it will pass forever while the thing it guards drifts. When a lock asserts on a rate, check that the fixture can produce a rate strictly between the extremes — an all-pass or all-fail fixture makes the assertion structurally unfalsifiable in the direction that matters.*

**Next:** fold the sixteen-fragment journal backlog, which now includes this one, and take the two THIRD-instance recurrences the curation pass surfaced — lesson 30 obliges a structural control in the same change.


### 2026-08-07 — Supersession is where goals go to disappear

*Plain summary: reviewing the day-old coder Definition of Done found ten stale or drifted claims
and five of the operator's own goal clauses with no capability row — the largest lost not by
argument but by supersession, when the DoD replaced the overhaul programme as the target and
never picked up the programme's biggest gap.*

The Lead Architect asked whether the coder Definition of Done was missing elements, needed more
criteria, or contained errors. The document had merged about half an hour earlier (`e7628176`,
16:54 local), so I expected the review to be mostly about structure. It was not. Three
verification agents (code, config/state ledgers, board) re-measured its load-bearing claims the
same evening, and the board sweep alone found five of its cited tickets had closed that same
afternoon, 15:15–17:02 local — three of them in the half-hour AFTER the document merged —
capability 7's "open" residuals list was wrong within hours of the document merging, and
capability 6's headline still said "instructed" above a correction block proving the instruction
had been reverted. Ten fixes shipped in `6b8e55db`. None of the document's CONCLUSIONS failed
verification — every error was in citations, headlines and board-lag, which is the document's own
thesis about statuses demonstrated on itself.

The judgment worth keeping came from mapping the operator's two quoted goal statements clause by
clause against the rows. Five clauses had no row: requirement coverage, media content,
difficulty-scaled candidates, graded decomposition, verdict stability. The first is the one that
teaches. On 2026-07-30 the overhaul programme named "the exam covers what was asked" the single
largest gap against "proper exams" — every GREEN ever produced is `GREEN-UNVERIFIED`. The
traceability instrument then SHIPPED (`green_quality/traceability.py`), and the DoD — written the
same day, replacing the programme as the thing agents check work against — never picked the goal
up. Not rejected, not deferred: orphaned by supersession, while its tickets (#967, #1015) kept
floating with no numbered capability to serve. Proposed lesson: **when a new document supersedes
an old one as the target, sweep the old one's claims for orphans before retiring it — a goal that
misses the handover does not fail, it silently stops being anyone's job.** The five rows are
PROPOSED (17-21) and queued for ratification with a status legend and a machine-check plan on
#1325, because the second finding was structural: nothing scans this file — no size budget, no
vocabulary sweep, no status grammar — so it rots at whatever rate the board moves.

Two suspected errors dissolved under verification and are recorded as REJECTED in the disposition
record: the "#1277 fixed / none of it exists" tension is the ticket's own precise language, and
the add-then-revert commit chain is exact to the minute. Verify-before-critique cost three agents
about ten minutes and converted the review from opinion into measurement; the rejected findings
are as load-bearing as the confirmed ones. Trade-off taken: the proposed rows went INTO the
document marked NOT RATIFIED (following the 11-16 pattern) rather than living only in the report
— visible where the next agent reads, at the price of a longer unratified section the operator
must now clear. Disposition record: docs/reviews/coder-dod-review-disposition-2026-08-07.md,
22/22 PASS. A live B9 pottery-site battery run (20260807-174202-bd, the operator's own goal
shape) started mid-review; ledgers were snapshotted at 17:48 before its artifacts could move them.

**Next:** the LA reads the review and ratifies or strikes 11-21 (#1325); the status-grammar gate
test ships after ratification; #1326 clears the two prose-contradicts-wiring defects once no
dispatch is reading the config.

### 2026-08-07 — Five failures, five causes, and two of them printed the same sentence

*Plain summary: the multi-page website test had never once run. Tonight it failed five times before it worked, and every failure was in the machinery around the coder rather than the coder itself. Two of them printed identical error text for completely unrelated reasons. It is now building the site — and the first task took 40 minutes while the next two took 8 and 11, which changes what we thought the whole thing costs.*

B9 is the card that measures the thing the operator says is his priority: a website with several pages that share a layout and a data source. It was authored on 2026-08-06 and had never been run. Tonight it ran five times.

**Attempt 1** died at the approve. I launched it with `Start-Process -WindowStyle Hidden` to dodge the harness's process reaper, and that context has no GPU. The boot log ends `opencl device count:2` followed by `no opencl gpu device is available` and `invalid gpu device in environment: index 0` — it *enumerated both devices and could use neither*. The model never loaded, the assistant accepted the socket and never answered. My mistake, against a rule I had written down myself: GPU work launches via an elevated scheduled task.

**Attempt 2** died in preflight, and this is where I made it worse. Attempt 1 had left an `ovms.exe` — the model server — resident. The box read **Available 0.05 GB of 31.32**, while the top ten processes summed to under one gigabyte, because the integrated GPU draws its VRAM from system memory and it appears in nobody's working set. I killed it, reclaimed 27 GB, and moved on satisfied.

**Attempt 3** died on a `Move-Item`, and taught the actual lesson. My cleanup had been half a cleanup: I killed the model server and left attempt 1's *swap driver* alive. That driver is detached by design — its own log says "the launcher is exiting now" — and it kept building in the sandbox until 18:39, an hour after the run that spawned it had exited. Attempt 3 archived that directory out from under it at 18:13. Both died: attempt 3 on a locked `public\` folder it could not move, and attempt 1's three candidates on `fatal: not a git repository: (NULL)` once their worktree's git pointer was emptied. They destroyed each other over one directory.

The transferable part is not "reap your orphans." It is that **I selected by memory footprint when ownership was the question**. Attempt 3's preflight read a clean 26.2 GiB and `nothing to lean — no restartable app was running`, while a live dispatch owned the very folder it was about to move. A swap driver mid-build is not a memory hog. Ownership was sitting on disk the whole time: a fleet journal with more `TASK-START` than `TASK-END` still owns its repo. That is the admission check now, and I verified it against the real artifact — at 18:13 that journal read 1 and 0, so it would have refused.

**Attempt 4** died at the approve again, with the *identical message* to attempt 1: `approve did not fire EXECUTE: No response from the Assistant Orchestrator`. Same sentence, entirely different cause. Attempt 4's assistant was healthy — zero GPU errors, 26 GiB free, egress guard armed. What killed it was that every battery run used the session key `"battery"`, a constant, and the coordinator parks its pending dispatch durably against that key. Attempt 1 created a pending plan, stalled, never cleared it; attempt 4 opened the same key and inherited it, then approved a plan whose sandbox had since been archived.

I had already seen the fingerprint and dismissed it. An hour earlier I filed a ticket noting that attempt 4's scorecard carried attempt 1's `run_id` — I called it a provenance nit and moved on. That borrowed id could only have come from the inherited pending slot. It was not a cosmetic defect standing near the real one; it *was* the real one, seen from the other end. Two tickets, one bug.

The fix is a per-run session key rather than a teardown step, and the reason matters more than the change: **the contaminating run had crashed**, so any cleanup it was supposed to perform would not have performed. Two runs that do not share a key cannot contaminate each other whatever either does.

**Attempt 5 is building the website.** Three tasks delivered and merged — home page, contact page, piece list — and the shared-layout task running. It is the first output this path has ever produced.

The measurement is the point, and it nearly fooled me. The card's eight-hour budget was extrapolated from an older card's "40–85 minutes per task" and had never been measured; replacing that guess was this run's stated first purpose. Task one took **39m43s** and I wrote it up as landing at the bottom of the expected range. Tasks two and three took **8m08s** and **10m58s**. The first task is a four-fold outlier paying one-off costs — model load, cold caches, scaffolding an empty repo — and had I banked the single sample I had, I would have concluded the opposite of the truth. The card's own instruction was to replace the extrapolation only after a full run produces a spread. It was right.

There is a smaller failure I want kept, because it is the same disease as everything else here. The operator told me, correctly, that I was not actively monitoring a live run — that hours can be lost before anyone notices. So I wrote a watchdog. Within the hour it fired STARVED at 0.08 GB free, which is the *expected* condition while a 30B model loads; it recovered to 4.99 unaided. And its stall check keyed off the fleet journal, which writes only `TASK-START` and `TASK-END` and therefore sits frozen for the entire build — a signal that reads identically whether the coder is working or dead, and so is useless in both directions. I built an instrument that could not distinguish the thing it claimed to detect, an hour after spending the day removing exactly that defect from other people's code. Progress is now measured by candidate writes; starvation needs two consecutive polls.

Then it died three times over: a trailing `&` detached it from its parent, a merge rewrote the script bash was mid-execution, and long-lived background shells were being reaped. I reported it armed while it was not. Believing a watchdog is running when it is not is worse than having none, which is the operator's original point arriving by a route I did not expect. It runs under the purpose-built monitor now.

**Proposed lesson:** *When two failures print the same message, assume two causes until each is separately explained. An error string names a symptom at the point it was noticed, never the condition that produced it — and the closer that string is to the top of a stack, the more distinct upstream faults it can represent.*

**Proposed lesson:** *A finding you classify as cosmetic while investigating a serious one deserves a second look before you file it away. The borrowed run id was the fingerprint of the defect I was hunting, and I filed it as a provenance nit in the same hour I was failing to find the cause.*

**Recurrence of lesson 225:** *gate the expensive resource on the cheapest probe of the experiment's validity precondition.* Three of five attempts were killed by a condition observable on disk in under a second before spending the GPU — an orphan holding memory, a live owner holding the sandbox, a pending slot holding a stale plan.

**Next:** the readiness probe half of #1324 is still open and still true — `AO up.` was logged 36 seconds after boot on every attempt tonight, including the ones that worked, and a 14B model does not load in 36 seconds. It did not break attempt 5, but it is a probe that cannot mean what it says.


### 2026-08-07 — Ratification by delegation, and the document that got truer by reading worse

*Plain summary: the Lead Architect ratified the Definition of Done extensions the same evening
they were proposed — "I approve of all the proposed ones that you think are good" — and applying
the ratified status vocabulary moved two capability rows DOWN, because honest words for statuses
make optimistic labels impossible.*

The review report merged at 18:54 local; within the hour the Lead Architect ratified the whole
proposed set — capabilities 11-21, the status legend, the goal-coverage map, the row-6/8
amendments — with the selection delegated to me: approve the ones the review judged good. I
began executing at 19:22. That delegation was only receivable
as a full-set ratification because every item already carried a written endorsement in the review
record; had one row been endorsed with reservations, the delegation would have forced a
follow-up question instead of an action. Proposed lesson: **a WHY-owner delegating WHICH to the
HOW-owner is only safe when the HOW-owner's judgment is already on the record item by item —
endorsements written before the delegation arrives are what make "the ones you think are good"
an executable instruction rather than a new decision.**

The mechanically interesting part of my evening was applying the legend to the ten ratified
rows. Two moved DOWN under my relabel: capability 5 (reviewers) from LIVE to ARMED, because what I could verify from July was the
decision function with real inputs, and the observable — a review-skip actually withdrawn inside
a live dispatch — has never been seen; capability 4 (exams) from LIVE to PARTIAL, half its proof
set being unreachable in production and its first measurement unvouched. Nothing about the
system changed tonight; only the words did, and the document now reads worse and is truer. The
enforcement landed in the same change (`tests/security/test_coder_dod_grammar.py`): every row
must carry a status, statuses must lead with a legend word, LIVE requires a dated citation, the
two acceptance tests must state their execution — and the checker is itself proven on synthetic
bad inputs, per the freshly-ratified capability 14 rule that a lock that has never been seen to
fail is not a lock. Trade-off taken: the grammar checks FORM, not truth — a fabricated "LIVE as
of" date passes it; the defense against that stays the re-measurement cadence and the
event-driven reviews, and the gate's docstring says so rather than implying more than it checks.
Executed under shared-tree pressure: another session held the primary checkout (feat/1327 plus
untracked work), so this arc ran from a dedicated worktree with junctioned model directories —
the first time the full standing gate ran gate-equivalent outside the primary tree.

**Next:** the operator-facing morning read picks up B9's processed=1-of-4 shortfall; #1326
clears the two prose-vs-wiring defects; the first measurement against ratified rows 17-21 needs
its own attribution window.

### 2026-08-08 — The instrument that blamed a person who was not there

*Plain summary: three of our tools reported a confident cause they had no way to know, and the operator caught the worst one by simply saying "I never did that" — which turned a slow build into a measured 25% defect.*

I opened today expecting to protect a battery night and spent it discovering that four
separate instruments were each asserting something their mechanism could not see. The
guard was the one I planned for. The other three found me.

The gate came first, and it paid for itself before I wrote a line. The landing card I
inherited said last night's 23:00 collision archived a live sandbox but that "nothing was
lost" — the archive is a rename, all twenty files intact. True, and it stops one step too
early. I read the night's own scorecard instead of the card's account of it. The nightly
had not merely collided; it had **stalled at 255.97 seconds** and written
`failure_class: HARNESS-BUDGET` against a **28,800-second** budget. Nine-tenths of one
percent of its budget, filed as running out of time. And the boot log showed the GPU was
healthy throughout — pipeline built on GPU at 23:01:05, the fourteen-billion-parameter
model resident at 23:01:14 — so the label was wrong about the cause *and* the machine was
fine. The collision had two casualties: a sandbox that was recoverable, and an eight-hour
measurement window that was not. The card recorded the first and not the second, which is
how a true sentence can still leave the next session with the wrong picture.

Then the guard, where I nearly shipped the same defect one level up. The ticket said to
extend the archive guard from task-level to run-level: it counts `TASK-START` against
`TASK-END`, those balance between waves, so go up a level. Journals do carry
`RUN-START`/`RUN-END`, so this looks right. I checked it against the run that actually
died — `20260807-195617-bd` — before building it. **RUN-START=6, RUN-END=6.** Balanced.
Those markers are emitted per *wave*, not per dispatch, so a run-level predicate would
have admitted the collision for precisely the reason the task-level one did. The journal
has no marker for the outer interval at any level; no reading of that file can answer the
question. What owns the sandbox for the whole run is the **process** driving it, and a
process is an interval by construction. I would have fixed the instance again and left the
class open for a third time, and the only thing that stopped me was measuring the obvious
fix before writing it.

The trade I rejected: relaxing `permission.external_directory` from `"ask"` to `"allow"`
would have cleared the day's other blockage in one line and immediately sped up a build
the operator was waiting on. It is a containment control, so it is his call and not mine —
but the honest reason not to propose it is that it treats the symptom. It would let the
coder reach the entire filesystem instead of pointing it at the right directory. I noted
it, recommended against it, and left the posture alone.

The instrument-honesty thread ran through everything and I was not exempt. The desktop app
told the operator **"BlarAI backend not running"** while the backend was verifiably serving
— it had asserted a whole-system verdict from one channel timing out, the mirror image of
#1324's port check claiming a readiness it could not see. My own watchdog then announced
his build had lost its backend, twice: once because I sampled a port at an instant while
the swap driver was legitimately restarting the model between waves, and once because I
treated `SUMMARY.txt` as a run-terminal marker when it is written every wave. **I made the
interval-vs-instant mistake twice, in instruments I built, on the same day I shipped a fix
for it.** Writing that down is the point. The class is not obscure and knowing it by name
did not stop me; only checking the marker against a completed run did.

The best finding of the day was not mine. Reading a coder log over my shoulder, the
operator said: *"i never rejected the permission. that's not true."* He was right. The
message is `The user rejected permission to use this specific tool call`, the run is
headless, and no human is in that loop at all. I had taken it at face value for several
minutes and gone looking for a permissions problem. What it actually meant: the coder was
sending `filePath: "/public/index.html"` — a leading slash, which resolves at the
filesystem root, lands outside the project, trips an `"ask"` rule, finds nobody to ask, and
is denied. Every read, write and edit in that attempt failed; **not one succeeded**. The
coder spent forty steps and seventeen minutes routing around it with `cat`, `cp` and
`echo`, which is exactly what its own instructions forbid — and it never saw the guidance
that would have helped, because the guidance did not exist. `AGENTS.md` warned only about
absolute paths *outside* the project, which reads as being about the home folder, and never
mentioned the one shape that always fails.

That fix is a documentation change, and it is the most valuable thing I shipped today.
Consecutive tasks of the same dispatch, same repo, same model, minutes apart:

| Task | Wall clock | Denials | Path shape |
|---|---|---|---|
| `home-page` (before) | 67.3 min | 11 | `/public/index.html` |
| `pieces-list-page` (after) | 50.2 min | 0 | fully drive-qualified, all of them |

Seventeen minutes and twenty-five percent, from one corrected sentence. I am deliberately
recording what that is *not* evidence of: the denial count 11 → 0 is exact and directly
attributable, but the minutes are confounded — task one also ran more visual-fix cycles,
and n=1 per arm. A 25% figure will get quoted, so the caveat belongs next to it.

Two things the guard work cost me that are worth naming. `b7af0a0` shipped sixty lines of
guard with **no test at all** — one file changed, nothing referencing it anywhere in the
repo — so nobody could have found the task-vs-run defect by running anything. An untested
control failed the way untested controls fail. And the existing taxonomy test asserted the
defect: `test_harness_attribution_routes_to_harness_budget` read `== CLASS_HARNESS_BUDGET`
and passed, so the bug was the recorded contract and three green sweeps never questioned
it. Baselines are evidence, and that one was evidence for the wrong thing.

The new verifier therefore has two modes and neither pretends to be the other. Its default
**refuses to run** while a dispatch is live, because the "nothing is running" cases cannot
be evaluated then, and passing them vacuously to reach a green total is the exact defect
the guard exists to stop. `-LiveProof` does the opposite and states in its result line what
it did not evaluate. It ran against the operator's real in-flight build — pid 22988, three
driving processes — which is a proof that was available today and will not be reproducible
tomorrow.

**Next:** merge the guard once his build ends, advance the pinned driver tree the 23:00
task actually runs from — a merge to `main` alone does not protect tonight — and re-verify
against that tree; then land the taxonomy split without advancing the measured tree, so
tonight carries exactly one battery-path change.

---

**Lesson proposals** (unnumbered — the integrator mints; search `LESSONS.md` first, and note
`LESSONS.md` is 1,772 bytes from its gate so #1319's size relief comes first):

1. An error message that names a CAUSE the mechanism cannot observe costs more than no
   message: "the user rejected permission" where no user exists, and "backend not running"
   from one channel's timeout, both sent readers to a fix that could not work. A tool may
   report what it observed and must not report who decided.
2. When a guard is defeated, measure the obvious next-level fix against the artifact that
   defeated it BEFORE building it — `RUN-START`/`RUN-END` looked like the level up and
   balanced 6-and-6 on the very run that died. Possible recurrence of the class that lesson
   30 owns; check before minting.
3. Operator-facing instructions are production surface, and their GAPS are defects: the
   coder failed for seventeen minutes on a path shape its instruction file never named,
   while the file did carry a rule it then broke. Check overlap with the
   documentation-is-not-evidence line before minting.

### 2026-08-08 — The exam that deleted the work

*Plain summary: a test that could never fail did not just mark a broken website as good — it convinced the planner the remaining work was already done, and a page he had asked for was never built. Two nights running. I only found it because I finally opened one of the sites.*

The finding of the day did not come from reading code. It came from opening a website.

B9 is the card that measures the thing the operator says is his priority — a pottery site with several pages sharing a layout and a data source. I had spent the afternoon convinced it had never produced anything, and I told him so twice. Both times I was wrong. It had completed **GREEN the night before**: six tasks merged, fourteen commits, thirty files. The product was sitting in `state/battery/night-20260807-230003/repos-archived/`, three levels inside a state directory, behind an archive step, where nobody would ever look at it. I found it only because a journal fragment I was reading quoted timings that did not match the run in front of me, and I checked rather than assuming.

So I copied it out and ran it. `node --test` gave twenty of twenty-one passing. Every page returned 200. The health endpoint answered. By every instrument this project owns, it was a working website.

It is missing a page he asked for.

His goal says, in his own words, *"a separate page for each individual piece with its own details and photo."* There is no such page, in either night's build. And the reason is not that the coder failed to write it. The reason is in the run record:

```
[skipped] create-piece-detail-page | already satisfied before wave 2:
          the job acceptance oracle PASSED on the current integrated tree
```

The job-level acceptance oracle is the highest-authority exam in the system — its own brief calls it *"the specification for this WHOLE multi-part goal"*, graded once on the final integrated project, protected from editing, restored before grading. It is thirty-three lines and holds five tests. Two have **empty bodies**, one carrying the comment *"we assume the pages are correctly created and accessible."* One calls the contact-form function and asserts nothing. One sets `piece.price = 15.99` and then asserts that `piece.price` is 15.99, on the same local object, discarding the return value of the function it claims to be testing. One filters an array inside the test with `Array.prototype.filter` instead of exercising the site's filter. It passes on any tree where its two imports resolve — including a tree where the product does not exist.

Here is the part I had not imagined, and it is why this is worth an entry rather than a ticket. **A worthless exam does not merely fail to catch a defect. It removes work from the plan.** An optimisation added months ago (#1049) runs the job oracle before dispatching a wave, so that a nine-minute coder candidate is never spent on something already done. The idea is sound and the implementation is scrupulously fail-closed — it refuses on a failed gate, on an exception, on a malformed result, on any status that is not `passed`. It checks everything except whether the oracle it is trusting was ever capable of saying no. Given a test that always passes, it concluded the remaining work was finished and skipped two tasks: the missing page, and — with a circularity I find genuinely unsettling — `acceptance-tests`, the task whose entire job was to write real acceptance tests. The fake exam certified that no better exam was needed.

Then the job graded GREEN and the battery banked a success.

The root cause is four lines. `f2p_seed_gate` returns early for any oracle whose path does not end in `.py`:

```python
if not rel_path.endswith(".py"):
    result.collectability = "skipped"
    result.f2p_baseline = "not-run"
    result.verdict = result.verdict or "seed"
    return result
```

The fail-to-pass baseline — run the exam against a skeleton where the product does not exist, and require it to FAIL — is the one check that catches an exam like this, and it is Python-only. A website is a node project. **The check that proves an exam can fail has never once run on the kind of work he cares most about.** And the record it left says `validated: true`, `findings_total: 0`, with `f2p_vacuous: 0` sitting directly beside `f2p_baseline: "not-run"`. Every reader takes `0 findings` to mean *we looked and found nothing*. It means *we did not look*.

I wanted to be sure this was a mechanism and not a bad night, so I did something I should do more often: I wrote the prediction down before it happened. At 17:42, with a run in flight and `integrate-styles` still building, I stamped on the ticket that when it finished, the pre-wave check would pass and `create-piece-detail-page` would be skipped again. At 18:21 it was. Second night, second independently authored plan, same missing page. That is no longer archaeology; it is a reproduced result. I was also wrong about two details in the same prediction — `acceptance-tests` was skipped for an ordinary dependency reason this time, and the run banked `PARKED-HONEST` rather than GREEN — and the second miss taught me more than the hit did: the amputation fires under either verdict. It does not need a GREEN to happen.

Two fixes went in. The narrow one makes the skip fail closed: an oracle that has never been shown capable of failing may not delete work. It deliberately fails in the *opposite* direction to its own sibling function three hundred lines above it, and the asymmetry is the whole design — `_oracle_unfit_from_run_dir` moves a verdict, so it fails soft toward "we cannot say", while this one decides whether work may be deleted, so it fails closed toward "not validated". Wrong permissively removes a page he asked for; wrong strictly costs one coder candidate. The wider fix gives node oracles a real fail-to-pass baseline and a static vacuity pass. Driven through the real seam rather than its fakes, the actual escaped oracle is now refused, and a legitimate hand-written one over the same module contract seeds clean; six deliberately awkward but honest shapes — regex literals full of braces, template strings, assertions made through a helper — produce zero findings, which is the direction that matters, because a false refusal blocks real work.

The thing I want to keep, though, is not the fix. It is that **four gates passed this site and one pair of eyes caught it in under a minute.** The operator asked me, in the middle of all this, for the outputs of the website batteries to be presented to him after each run so he could evaluate and give feedback — and he was asking for precisely the instrument that had been missing. Every grader in this pipeline measures whether an instrument ran honestly. Not one measures whether it was *right*. The document has a row for that (capability 12, the gates' false-negative rate) and it has read `UNKNOWN` since the day it was written, because its ground truth is him looking at the thing. This is the first entry in that column: one delivery, one escaped defect, four gates.

So the loop is built — a launcher that finds every site the coder has made, including the archived ones that would otherwise vanish by morning, serves it, and shows it beside what the run claimed about it, leading with what the run did *not* build. Then it asks him what is wrong and saves his words against the run id.

And it caught me too. Verifying it, I found that its port probe bound `127.0.0.1` while node binds `::`, so an occupied port read as free; the review server died with `EADDRINUSE` and the *previous* review's process answered every request. The page presented as tonight's site would have been last night's, with every line of output claiming success. That is the disease of the entire day, committed by the tool built to diagnose it, inside an hour of my writing the ticket about it. The fix plants a token in the served tree and refuses to show him anything it cannot prove is the right build — but the lesson is that "I have just been thinking hard about this exact failure" is worth nothing as a defence. Only the check is.

**Next:** wire the presentation into the battery night so he does not have to remember to look, and close the loop's other half — his words reaching the next run, which needs the resume path (#1321).

### 2026-08-08 — The exam that could not fail, in the ecosystem he cares about most

*Plain summary: the test that decides whether a whole website job succeeded was never itself checked for node projects — so a five-question exam with two blank questions passed, and the build quietly deleted a page he had asked for. Node exams are now checked the same way Python ones already were.*

I went looking for why B9 shipped a pottery site with no individual-piece page and still graded GREEN, and the answer was four lines of code that had been sitting in `shared/fleet/oracle_qa.py` since #821. The seed gate — the thing that decides whether an acceptance oracle is fit to grade a job — opened with `if not rel_path.endswith(".py")`, stamped `collectability: skipped` and `f2p_baseline: not-run`, and returned `seed`. Every non-Python oracle skipped every execution stage. The docstring said so plainly, as intent: *"there is no pytest for a `.mjs` oracle."* That is true and it is not a reason. There is `node --test`, the dispatch driver already grades with it, and node v24 has been on this box the whole time.

The consequence is the part worth keeping. Websites are node jobs, and websites are what the operator says he wants most. So the one ecosystem he named was precisely the one where the highest-authority exam in the system was never proven able to fail. The escaped artifact is 33 lines: five tests, two with empty bodies, one that calls `storeEnquiry` and asserts nothing, one that sets `piece.price = 15.99` and then asserts `piece.price === 15.99`, one that filters an array with `Array.prototype.filter` and checks the result is non-empty. It passes on any tree where its two imports resolve. `oracle-qa.json` recorded `validated: true, findings_total: 0` beside `f2p_baseline: not-run` — the honest field sitting next to the confident one, lending it credibility.

The thing I got wrong first, and the reason I measured before building: I assumed a fail-to-pass baseline would catch this. It does not. I ran the real oracle against a real greenfield skeleton and node reported `tests 1, pass 0, fail 1` — the whole file dies at import resolution and collapses into ONE failing aggregate, which is a clean `all-fail`. The execution stage is blind to this oracle on exactly the tree where it matters. Had I built only what the shape of the Python path suggested, I would have shipped a gate that returned `all-fail` on the artifact that escaped and called it done — a second instrument reporting a measured pass it never measured, which is the defect class I was there to close. What convicts it is a static pass: two empty test bodies, detectable without executing anything. So the change ships both stages, deliberately independent, and the module comment says why neither subsumes the other.

Calibrating the static detector was the real design work, and the brief was explicit that a false REFUSE is much worse than a missed vacuous test, because a false refuse blocks real work. Two shapes convict: an empty body, and a body with no assertion AND no first-party call. I deliberately left the third alone. An assertion-free test that DOES call a first-party symbol is a bare no-raise smoke test — it fails on a skeleton because the call throws, so it genuinely discriminates. This module already knew that: `_exercises_first_party` carries a docstring saying a no-raise first-party call counts, written so "a legitimate smoke oracle passes the floor and is not false-flagged." Hard-refusing that shape statically would have contradicted a rule authored to prevent a false flag. It is caught by measurement instead, wherever the skeleton lets the imports resolve. I also left the tautology shape (`piece.price = 15.99` … `assert.equal(piece.price, 15.99)`) undetected, and that one cost me the most thought: it has a legitimate twin. "Set a field, call the product, assert the field is unchanged" is how you test that a function does not mutate its input. I could not separate the two without a false-positive class I was unwilling to ship, so it stays uncaught and stays written down here.

Then I tested the calibration against evidence rather than against my own confidence. Twenty-one real files — eleven historical job oracles from `state/fleet-runs/` and ten coder-written test files from the B9 project — through the detector. Zero false positives. One flag: the escaped oracle, with exactly its two empty bodies. That run also turned up something I was not looking for. Two oracles (`20260806-094526-bd` and `20260806-171303-bd`) are truncated mid-statement at exactly 6306 bytes, five open braces against four closed, ending on `const habits55 = await get`. Both were seeded as-is, because the node path ran no stage that could look. They now refuse on a `node --check` parse failure, which is the third stage I added and the one I had not planned: node can give a genuine collectability verdict without executing anything, so the flat `skipped` stamp had no excuse either.

The asymmetry the Python path established is preserved exactly: a machinery failure (no node, a timeout, a runner that dies before TAP speaks) is a fail-soft `not-run` with no finding, while a confirmed pre-implementation pass is fail-closed. I checked the single consumer, `_oracle_qa_seed_gate` in `swap_ops.py`, before changing the verdict node can return: it branches only on `verdict == "refuse"`, and nothing in the tree keys off `collectability == "skipped"`. Making node capable of refusing breaks no caller.

What I have not done, and it belongs to items 1 and 2 of #1342 rather than here: `already_satisfied` still trusts a passing oracle without checking whether that pass was measured. This change means a vacuous node oracle no longer reaches it. It does not mean an unmeasured pass has stopped being treated as authority.

**Next:** the `blarai-wt-1342-precheck` limb closes the `already_satisfied` half; once both land, the predicate on #1342 — a B9 run where a vacuous job oracle is refused and `create-piece-detail-page` is dispatched rather than skipped — becomes observable on a single night.

### 2026-08-08 — The cheapest instrument in the building was a person clicking a link

*Plain summary: he looked at a website the system built and found three things wrong in about a minute that four automated gates had passed. The most useful thing I built afterwards was forty lines of code that follows every link and says which ones lead nowhere — and it caught the same defect he did, in a tenth of a second.*

He opened one of the websites. That is the whole event, and it reorganised the day.

Seven observations came back. Three were real defects. The same photograph reused across different products; descriptions that did not match the picture beside them; and every "View Details" button on the gallery leading to `{"error":"not found"}`. I verified all three on disk before writing any of it down, because his technical claims are hypotheses like anyone's — and all three held. He also noticed what was working, unprompted, which is worth recording because a reviewer who only reports faults is a reviewer being polite: the images were good, the pages were laid out well, the prose was varied rather than templated, and the contact form rejected bad input properly.

That third defect is the one that matters. It is the same missing page I had spent the afternoon tracing through source — an acceptance oracle that could not fail, consumed by an optimisation that concluded the remaining work was already done, so the page was deleted from the plan. I found it by reading run records, cross-checking a prediction, and eventually convicting four lines of code. **He found it by clicking a button.** The instrumentation took hours; the eyeball took under a minute. I do not think the right lesson is that instrumentation is overrated. I think it is that the two find *different* things, and this project had built a great deal of one and none of the other.

So the first thing I built was the check that needed no oracle and no model at all: follow every `href` and `src` in the delivered site and ask whether the target exists. Two decisions in it were forced by the real artifact rather than by my design sense, and both would have gone the other way if I had reasoned from the shape of the problem.

The first: split the query string off *before* testing whether a reference is decidable. The escaped link is `piece-detail.html?id=${piece.id}`. It contains an interpolation, so the obvious rule — "give up on anything with `${}`" — classifies it as unknowable and passes the site clean. But the path component before the `?` is a plain literal. Getting that ordering backwards produces a checker that passes the exact delivery it was written to catch, which is a specific enough failure that I wrote a toggle-proof test to pin the ordering in place.

The second I only found by running it against the real archived site instead of a fixture. It reported one broken link. There are two — the same anchor appears in `app.js` and again inside an inline `<script>` in `pieces.html`. An HTML parser is *required* to treat script content as opaque text, so my parser-only scan found the JavaScript copy and was structurally blind to the identical one four files away. Half a defect, reported as a whole. Had I tested against markup I wrote myself, I would have written markup my parser could see.

Then the checker caught me the way the review tool caught me the night before. Its first sweep across every web project on this box returned, for four empty scaffolds, `checked: true, hard: false` — zero findings, no problems. Those directories contain a README. **A scan that examined nothing had produced a clean bill of health**: precisely the `validated: true / findings_total: 0` shape I had spent the previous eight hours convicting, committed by the instrument built to convict it, within an hour of writing the ticket. It now reports `checked: false` and publishes no counts at all when it scanned no files. I keep learning the same thing in new costumes: *having just thought hard about a failure is worth nothing as a defence against committing it.* Only the check is worth anything, and the check has to be run against reality rather than against your intentions.

One decision I deliberately did not make. A broken link **reports**; it does not fail a build. His instruction was that a bad link means "the delivery is reported", and a brand-new way for a build to go red is a change in what the system does — his call, not a wiring decision I make while I happen to be in the file. There is a regression lock proving a broken site and a clean site produce identical verdicts today, so the day someone promotes this to a gate, they will have to do it on purpose.

The other half of the day was aimed at the sentence in his review I keep coming back to: *"I am not really sure what I am supposed to expect, to be honest with you."* That is not a gap in him. His verdict is the only ground truth this project has for whether its gates are any good, and I had asked him to produce one while showing him nothing to measure against. So the review page now carries his own goal cut into the nine separate things he asked for, in his own words, verbatim — a check proves each clause is a genuine substring of the goal the run was actually given, so it cannot quietly drift into requirements-speak. Four of the nine ask for more than one thing, and those prompt on both halves; the second half ("*instead of just losing what they typed*") is exactly the sort that passes unnoticed. And there are three answers, not two, because an instrument that forces a verdict manufactures one. "I could not tell" carries the same weight as yes and no, and a fourth state records clauses he never reached — collapsing those two would let a review he abandoned after two questions read afterwards as nine considered answers.

Both subagents came back having found things by verifying rather than asserting, which is the habit I care about most. One noticed that the state directory it wanted to write clauses into was gitignored, so the file would have been uncommittable and gone on the first wipe. The other handed its generated launcher to a real `cmd.exe` and discovered that a tick character in a skip message would render as mojibake in the one window he actually looks at.

**Next:** the 23:00 battery is the first live test of the amputation fix, and the question is single and sharp — does the new B9 run *build* `create-piece-detail-page`, or skip it as already satisfied for a third consecutive night? If it builds it, the link check should come back clean on a delivered site for the first time, and he gets a review page with nothing red on it that he did not put there himself.

### 2026-08-08 — A zero is not a number until you know who counted it

*Plain summary: the record that graded the websites said "0 problems found" when what it meant was "we did not look." I did not change a single count — I made the counts readable, so that the two can never again look the same. Then I ran the new link checker over every website this system has ever built and found a site nobody had ever opened whose stylesheet is missing.*

The line I keep returning to from this morning's finding is not the vacuous oracle. It is this pair of fields, sitting two lines apart in the same JSON object:

```
"findings": { "f2p_vacuous": 0, ... },
"f2p_baseline": "not-run",
```

`f2p_vacuous` **is** the check for vacuous tests. It reported zero against an exam that was three-fifths vacuous. The reason is not that it looked and missed them; it is that the stage which produces that finding never ran. And the honest field admitting so was *right there*, adjacent, in plain sight — and did nothing, because nobody reads a record field by field. They read the headline, and the headline said zero.

That is what makes this class dangerous rather than merely wrong. The unmeasured result is not disguised. It is **accompanied** by its own disclaimer, and the disclaimer's presence makes the whole record look scrupulous. An honest field standing next to a confident one lends the confident one credibility. I have now found this shape four times in two days, and every time the disclosure was already present and already ignored.

So the fix changes no count at all. It makes a count **interpretable**. Every finding class now names the stage that produces it — vacuity comes from the fail-to-pass baseline, collectability from the load probe, traceability from the coverage map, the rest from the static scan — and a single field, `findings_conclusive`, is false whenever any producing stage did not run. The reasoning underneath is asymmetric and worth stating plainly: an unrun check can only ever have *missed* something, never over-reported it, so a total from an incomplete scan is a floor and not a count. Calling it a count is the error.

Re-emitting this morning's exact stamps now produces `findings_conclusive: false`, a status of `partial`, and three sentences naming what did not happen. A fully measured run produces `true` and an empty list. Those two records were previously **byte-indistinguishable in every field a reader looks at**, which is the entire explanation for how a missing page survived four gates.

I also retired a second copy of a vocabulary while I was in there. The set of stamps meaning "did not run" was declared privately inside `swap_driver`, the consumer, rather than inside `oracle_qa`, which writes them. Two copies of a vocabulary drift, and this one would have drifted in the direction that hurts: a stamp the consumer failed to recognise as unmeasured would be read as evidence, and an unvalidated oracle would be free to delete work from the plan again. The module that writes a word owns what it means.

Then the part that actually reaches him. Making a record honest is worth very little if the record is read by machines and the person deciding whether to trust the delivery never sees it. His review page now answers one question in his own register — *was the marking any good?* — and for the site he looked at, the answer is: **the exam that graded this was never shown able to fail, so your eyes are the only real check on this delivery.** Three renderings, never two: validated, not-validated, and no-record-at-all, with the last saying *unknown* rather than *fine*. The subtle part is the fallback for records written before the new field existed, and it took its own toggle-proof to get right, because the tempting implementation reads a missing field as a pass. The absence of the field is the absence of the check.

The day's last measurement was the cheapest and, I suspect, the most useful. I pointed the new link checker at every tree this fleet has ever produced: 209 candidates, of which 31 actually contain pages. 128 links resolve, 8 lead nowhere, 3 sites carry a hard finding. Two of those three I already knew about. The third I did not: `authorwebsite3000`, which nobody has ever opened, loads `header/header.styles.css` — a file that is not in the delivered tree. **The site renders unstyled.** It has four working links and four broken ones. That is not a subtle defect; it is the first thing anyone would notice, and it shipped, and it sat there.

The sweep also vindicated the one design decision I was least sure about. Three of that site's four breaks are extensionless paths like `/about-me`, which might be routes the server builds at runtime. The checker grades those LOW and says it cannot tell; only the missing stylesheet, which no routing story rescues, is HIGH. Had I flagged all four, the gate would be nagging a coder about three things that may be perfectly correct, and a gate that cries wolf stops being read. The instrument's usefulness lives entirely in the gap between what it knows and what it merely suspects, and in being willing to say which is which.

Worth keeping in proportion, though: of the seven things he observed on the pottery site, this instrument would have caught **one**. The repeated photographs and the descriptions that do not match their pictures need a grader that does not exist yet. A cheap automatic check does not replace the person looking at the thing. It narrows what the person has to look for, and that is a smaller claim than I would like to be making.

**Next:** the 23:00 battery, and the single question of whether `create-piece-detail-page` gets built or skipped for a third night. Whatever happens, the run's own record will now say whether its exam was ever checked — so if it grades itself GREEN, that GREEN arrives with its provenance attached for the first time.

### 2026-08-08 — Seven observations, and the line between what a cheap check can see and what it cannot

*Plain summary: he made seven observations about a website in about a minute. I went through them one at a time and asked which could be checked by a program that never opens an image and never runs the site. Four could. The instruments now say so — and, more importantly, they say out loud which three they cannot.*

The temptation after his review was to build one big grader. What actually helped was taking his seven observations literally and sorting them by what would be needed to catch each one.

Two were pure filesystem questions. *"View Details leads to a JSON error"* — does the file exist? *"The same images were used over and over"* — how many distinct pictures do six products point at? Neither needs a model, a browser, or a pixel. Both are now checked in about a tenth of a second, and both fire on the real delivery.

One was almost checkable and turned out to be the interesting one. *"Descriptions of the products don't match the image."* You cannot compare a description to a photograph without looking at the photograph. But you can notice that three bowls are rendering a file called `hero.png` — and the reason that convicts is not a subject comparison at all. `hero.png` names no subject; nothing about the filename says *not a bowl*. What convicts it is that the asset is page **furniture**, and a product is wearing it. That is a different question from "does this picture show a bowl", and it happens to be answerable.

Four remain out of reach: whether a picture is any good, whether the prose is any good, whether the layout holds at phone width, whether the whole thing feels like a shop. Those are his.

The design decision I spent longest on was the repeat threshold, and it is worth writing down because the reasoning generalises. Two products sharing a photograph is a coin flip — colour variants, a boxed pair, one shot of a series. Three is a *pattern*, and a pattern in generated content is a pipeline defect rather than a coincidence: the generator produced fewer subjects than the data declares and the placer reused what it had. Setting it at two would have bought recall at the cost of precision, and a gate that nags a coder about correct markup stops being read. But the more important call was refusing to solve the logo problem with a threshold at all. A number tuned high enough to ignore a logo appearing on five pages would rot the first time somebody sold six of something. Instead the rule counts only assets an *item declares in the data source*, so page furniture is out of scope by construction. A structural exclusion cannot be undone by the next site's shape; a tuned one can.

The same instinct applied to the honesty rendering, which is now the part of this work I would defend hardest. Every one of these checks has four states on his page — found something, found nothing, could not run, was not run — and the last two say *unknown* rather than *fine*. Even the clean rendering states its own limit: *this check never opens an image*. That sentence costs nothing and buys the only thing that matters, which is that when a check says clean he can believe exactly what it says and no more. A page that let him read "no picture problems" as "the pictures are right" would be trading his trust for a nicer-looking report, which is the same currency this project has been overspending all week.

The gate caught me twice today, which is the correct number of times for a day with this much output. The first: a status line I wrote into the Definition of Done reading `Status: STARTED`, invented because it described how the evidence felt. STARTED is not in that document's legend, the grammar gate rejected it, and `PARTIAL` was the honest word anyway — one reviewed delivery is a data point, not the published rate the row asks for. A status invented to fit the evidence is a claim escaping the vocabulary that makes it checkable, which is precisely the defect class I spent the day building instruments against, aimed at the document rather than at a grader. The second: the size budget, breached by 284 bytes, because I had written today's arcs into the snapshot as though they were still news. They merged. They are history, and history lives in the journal and the tickets. What a successor needs from #1342 is not the story — it is the open half and tonight's observable.

Both subagents found real defects in their own first passes and said so unprompted. One discovered that grading a merged item list would report a false repeat on any repo shipping its catalogue twice, a shape these scaffolds actually produce. The other found that the tick character in a real skip message renders as mojibake in the console window he actually reads, because `cmd.exe` opens a `.cmd` in the OEM codepage. Neither would have been visible without running the real thing against the real artifact, and neither was in anybody's design. *(Folded 2026-08-09: the mojibake finding also appears in "The cheapest instrument in the building was a person clicking a link" above. Two review rounds the same evening surfaced it independently; both accounts are kept because each records what its own round found, and which round noticed a defect first is exactly the sort of detail a tidied journal loses.)*

**Next:** 23:00, and the question that has been the same since this morning — does `create-piece-detail-page` get built, or skipped for a third consecutive night? Tomorrow's morning report will name the site either way, in his own words, with the file to double-click.

### 2026-08-08 — The instrument that called his photographs a mistake

*Plain summary: I built a checker that finds generated pictures no page uses, ran it over everything, and reported that 54 images were wasted. Twenty-nine of them were his own family recipe photos, which he had put there himself because the system told him to. The real number was 11.*

I had the number for fifteen minutes and it was on a ticket before I found out it was wrong.

The picture checker had a rule I was pleased with: an image in the delivered tree that nothing references is generation nobody asked for, and on a machine where overnight graphics time is the scarcest resource that is a real cost. I ran it across every tree the system has ever produced. Fifty-four orphans. I found the measured per-image cost — 59.9 seconds, recorded on this hardware in June — multiplied it out, got 54 minutes of wasted GPU time, and filed it.

Then, because one tree accounted for 29 of the 54, I opened it.

`recipe-site` contains a README saying *"a website for our family recipes"*, one initial commit, no code at all, and twenty-nine photographs. And a second README, written by the project scaffold, sitting in the folder those photographs are in: *"Put photos, documents, and other material for the build in here; screenshots to emulate go in the reference folder."*

They are his photographs. He put them there because the system told him to. My instrument counted them as the machine's waste.

The number was wrong by five times, and that is the smaller half. **An instrument that tells someone their own uploads were a mistake is worse than no instrument**, and it fails in the precise way I had spent the same evening writing warnings about: a false positive in a gate is not a nuisance, it is the thing that makes the gate stop being read. I had written that sentence into two module docstrings before committing it in a third.

The fix had one interesting decision in it. The obvious exclusion is "skip directories called `assets`", and it is wrong, because generated media lives in a directory called `assets` too — the pottery site keeps its real, genuinely-orphaned pictures in `public/assets/`. A name-based exclusion would have quietened the instrument on exactly the case it was built for. What distinguishes the two is not the name but the marker: the scaffold *wrote a file* into the intake folder explaining what it is for, so the exclusion can rest on something checkable rather than on a convention I would be guessing at. The regression lock that matters is not the one proving his photos are spared; it is the one proving an `assets/` directory with no marker is still graded normally.

The other six false positives were screenshots in a `testing_output/` folder. Between them, the two classes were five-sixths of my headline.

What I want to keep is the shape of how both errors were caught, because it was the same move twice within an hour and neither time was it cleverness. Earlier I had been about to multiply a per-image cost of "about 76 seconds" across every delivery — a figure I had taken from a subagent's report and put on a ticket without checking. I went to find its source before multiplying, and there was a real measurement two directories away saying 59.9. Then I went to check whether the other trees shared `recipe-site`'s shape before writing more, and they did not. Both times the check was *stop and go read the thing*, and both times it changed the answer.

The uncomfortable part is that the first version of each claim was already written down and already published to a ticket the operator reads. Neither error was caught by a gate, a test, or a review. They were caught because I kept going after I had a result I liked, and I do not have a mechanism that guarantees that — only a habit, which is precisely what lesson 33's third instance obliged me to replace with a control earlier the same evening. The control I shipped catches an instrument that publishes a total with no way to say a check did not run. It would not have caught either of these.

One thing genuinely worth having came out of the wreckage. `recipe-site` is not a waste finding; it is a different one entirely. **He gave the system twenty-nine photographs for a family recipe website, the intake path worked exactly as designed, and nothing was ever built.** The `reference/` folder beside them — for screenshots of sites to emulate — is empty. That belongs to the interview and intake work, and it is the most concrete evidence available of what that path actually produces in his hands.

**Next:** the traceability limb, then 23:00.

### 2026-08-08 — Eighty-one runs, and the check that matters most never ran once

*Plain summary: I went looking for why requirement coverage was empty on websites, found a second instrument with the same blind spot, and then — reading every grading record this project has ever written — found that the single most important check in the whole system has never produced a result. Not on one night. Not on one language. Eighty-one out of eighty-one.*

This started as a small errand. Capability 17 — does the exam cover what was asked? — has read ARMED for weeks: instrument shipped, never fired. I wanted to know why.

The answer was four characters. `tools/dispatch_harness/green_quality/traceability.py` looks for the seeded exam with `glob("job-oracle-*.py")`. B9's exam is `job-oracle-acceptance.job.test.mjs`. So the assessor found nothing and recorded:

```
"available": false, "reason": "no seeded job oracle in the run dir"
```

The oracle is in that directory. I listed it. **The reason is not incomplete, it is false** — it tells a reader the run never seeded an exam, so anyone chasing empty coverage goes hunting for a broken seeding step that works perfectly. That is worse than the ecosystem gap it is hiding, and it is the third instrument this week caught saying something true-sounding that points the wrong way.

Both halves are fixed now, and the number that came out is worth quoting. On the real B9 run: `oracle_coverage=0/6 L3+ (weak:1 gap:0 undecidable:5)`. Six operator requirements; **five have no assertion in the exam that even names their subject**.
