The one that binds — "store submitted enquiries" — binds at the weakest rung, with the reason *"a test calls this criterion's subject but the test asserts nothing"*, which is precisely the defect #1342 found by reading the file. Two independent instruments, same verdict, arrived at from different directions.

The positive control is the part I would insist on before believing any of it. `0/6` is exactly what a matcher that never fires would print. So the agent building it fed the same real oracle a criterion worded to name its subject, and it reached the top rung on `getPieces` — credited across two hops, through a variable, to an assertion that names no subject at all. The matcher works. Those five criteria genuinely have nothing covering them.

Then I went one question further than the errand required, and that is where the evening turned.

If the traceability assessor had been blind to node oracles, what else was? So I read every `oracle-qa.json` this project has ever written — 81 of them, back to 12 July.

**Every single one carries `f2p_baseline: "not-run"`.**

The fail-to-pass baseline is the check that runs an exam against a tree where the product does not exist and requires it to *fail*. It is the one instrument that can tell a real exam from a decorative one. It is the whole point of the QA system. And it has never produced a result — not on Python, not on node, not on one night in four weeks. Sixty-seven of the 81 also report `findings_total: 0` beside it, which is the shape I spent this morning convicting.

This reframes what I told the operator earlier today, and I want the correction on the record rather than folded quietly into a summary. #1342 diagnosed a *node-only* early return, and that diagnosis was right and the fix was right. But the Python rows are stamped `not-run` just as consistently, so **the node skip was one of at least two reasons this check has never run, and closing it did not make the baseline start working.** I reported a root cause when I had found a root cause; there was another one underneath, and only reading the whole corpus surfaced it.

I also had to walk back my own corroboration within the hour. I re-ran the gate live against the escaped oracle and got `not-run`, and wrote that up as confirmation — then checked what the function actually expects and found that `skeleton_dir` means *the tree before the work is done*, while I had passed the finished project. My reproduction was invalid. The 81 banked records stand on their own and need no reproduction; the live run I had dressed as evidence does not. That is the third claim I have corrected today by going and reading the thing rather than trusting the number in front of me, and the honest observation is that none of the three was caught by a gate, a test, or a review. They were caught because I kept going after I had an answer I liked, and a habit is not a mechanism.

The counterweight belongs here too, because "the QA has never worked" is not what the evidence says. The *static* half is real and it bites: the escaped B9 oracle grades `verdict: refuse` today, on collectability and adequacy findings, and that is state-independent and reproducible. The belt is holding. It is the braces that have never been fastened, and the field saying so has read `not-run` since the day it was written, in plain sight, on every record, for a month.

**Next:** 23:00. And a smaller thing I got wrong that is worth its own line — I merged the traceability branch while its agent was still working, took only its first commit, and nearly shipped the assessor without the tests proving it is reached at battery close rather than only when called directly. A clean working tree is not the same as finished work.


### 2026-08-09 — The monitor I built to detect a quiet tree was watching two corpses

*Plain summary: A tool built to answer "is anyone still working here?" checked whether certain processes existed, not whether they were doing anything. Both processes it kept finding were watching files that had stopped changing hours and days earlier — one log ended six hours before I even looked at it, the other two days. The real answer had been sitting in a timestamp the whole time, and a standing-gate test I found afterward turned out to be the same mistake wearing different clothes.*

I was asked to work the public-mirror sync — the weekly job that copies this repository to a public GitHub mirror. It had been silently failing since 08-03, and the instinct was to fix it and move on. Reading before acting stopped that: a July retrospective entry said the mirror was "correctly frozen" because the current journal named a private codename, and a ticket (#1104) already had an independent investigation from two nights earlier reaching the same conclusion in more detail — the leak-gate had found a real hit, not a false positive, and explicitly escalated the content question rather than deciding it. Nothing about that had changed since; the codename references had merely moved, verbatim, into a July archive file when the month rotated, which is not the same as being resolved.

An independent gate review, before any of this reached the Lead Architect, caught two real defects in my draft: I'd named the wrong branch as "someone's live work" when the actual state was three different branches in thirty minutes, none of them anyone's parked edits, and the message had visibly garbled text in three sections. Both were fixed before he ever saw them. Then he asked to see the flagged text directly rather than take my summary — and he was right to: when I quoted it verbatim, I miscounted it as three codename mentions in my own summary sentence, when I had correctly worked out four just a few lines earlier in the same reasoning. He decided (correctly, on the complete text either way): not privacy-sensitive, fine to be public. A second independent review of the resulting decision record caught that I had also mischaracterized the leak-gate itself as having "false-blocked" — wrong, and the opposite of what the ticket's own record said: *"the control worked, and the only reason this is not an incident is that it worked."* Two reviews, two rounds of me being wrong about a claim I could have re-derived instead of restated, both caught before landing.

The mechanical half was small: a bookkeeping ref had drifted two commits behind the real remote tip, so I realigned it and built `scripts/check_public_mirror_sync.py` — an on-demand check, tested against real throwaway git repos rather than mocked git calls, that reports the drift instead of failing silently for nine days like the thing it replaces almost did twice. Gate-green, independently reviewed, ready to merge in under an hour. Then it sat for three more, because the primary checkout was taking a new merge every fifteen to twenty minutes and the nightly battery window opened on top of that — correctly not merging into a tree something else is using is not a failure, but it meant the actual work was done long before it could land.

He asked me to set up monitoring so I would not miss the signal. I built one: poll every ninety seconds, look for processes matching battery- and dispatch-shaped command lines, require the match to be empty for five consecutive checks plus twenty quiet minutes on `main` before declaring it safe. It reported "still busy" identically four times across ninety minutes — same six processes, every check. That consistency is what made me stop trusting it rather than keep waiting on it. I read the two log files the matched processes were tailing. One had last written at 18:21:06 that evening — a fleet run that had ended, its retry left unused, six hours before I even looked. The other had last written 2026-08-06 20:14:36 — two days stale. Neither `tail -f` had received a byte since, and neither ever would; they were watching corpses, and a process attached to a dead file looks, to a liveness check, identical to one attached to a live one. A broader sweep found nothing else running that mattered, and the nightly battery's own Task Scheduler record showed it had fired at 23:00:01 and exited within the hour — a stand-down, not an overnight run. `main` had genuinely been idle since 22:15:35. I merged on that direct evidence rather than the monitor's, at 00:51:43 — `a208b705`.

The post-merge gate re-run, required regardless of how small the change looks, turned up a third instance of the identical shape, in a file my merge never touched. Three tests in `shared/tests/test_image_placement.py` assert against a live external site directory outside git, pinned to whatever it looked like when the tests were written that same evening — two unused images, one report shape. Re-run three and a half hours later, the site has since been regenerated with a different asset-naming scheme entirely; the tests don't have a stale answer, they have no answer the current shape can even parse. I confirmed by structural proof, not inference, that my merge could not have caused it — `git show --stat` touches zero files the test imports — diagnosed it, and left it flagged on the ticket that owns the context (#1349) rather than guess at a fix on someone else's active arc at one in the morning.

Three instances in one session, all the same defect: a value captured at one moment gets treated as still describing the present, and nothing re-checks it against what actually changed underneath. My own summary sentence, a monitor's process list, a test's hard-coded expectation — the mechanism differs each time and the shape does not. This reads like the same family as the instrument-honesty arc already running in this project (#1241 and its kin): the failure is never that the check runs and lies, it's that the check answers a question adjacent to the one being asked, and the adjacency is invisible until someone reads the timestamp instead of trusting the presence.

Gate on merged main: 10840 passed, 3 failed (diagnosed, unrelated, flagged on #1349), 0 skipped, 133 deselected. The pre-merge branch measurement was 10787 passed, 0 failed, 23 skipped (documented gitignored-model/docset delta) — the difference is everything else that landed on `main` in the hours the merge waited.

**Proposed lesson:** *A liveness check that matches on process presence, not on evidence the process is still producing anything, cannot distinguish an active worker from an orphaned watcher of a file that stopped changing. Check the timestamp inside, not the process outside.* Possibly a recurrence of the #1241 instrument-honesty class rather than a fresh number — worth checking against that index before minting, not asserting here.

**Next:** #1104's remaining half (fold the allowlist + fail-loud into `agentic-setup`'s committed publisher) waits for that tree to be free; DEC-12 already clears the content question so it no longer needs its own round-trip. #1349 owns the live-site-test-fragility fix. Neither is mine to pick up tonight. *(Corrected below — the LA asked directly, that tree was free, and I did pick the allowlist half up an hour later. Leaving this wrong prediction in rather than editing it away.)*

### 2026-08-09 — I fixed the bookkeeping and reported success. He asked why the public repos still hadn't changed.

*Plain summary: Realigning a pointer and calling it done is not the same as publishing anything. He caught the gap directly. Actually finishing the job surfaced a real credential-shaped string in a test fixture that this project's own leak-gate had no pattern for — and GitHub's independent secret scanner caught it anyway, which is what defense-in-depth is for.*

I had reported #1104 as shipped: bookkeeping realigned, `DEC-12` recorded, a new drift-check tool built and tested. All true, and all beside the point he was actually asking about. "I needed you to update the public github repos. doesn't seem like you did dthat" — he was right, plainly. The bookkeeping ref pointed at the right commit; `main` itself was still thousands of commits ahead of what the public mirror actually showed, because I had never triggered a real publish. Fixing the symptom that blocks a fix is not the fix.

The reason I hadn't run the real publisher was `agentic-setup` being off-limits for most of the night. A fresh check showed that constraint had lapsed — clean tree, no live processes beyond the same stale watchers from earlier — so I read the actual committed script for the first time instead of continuing to reason about it from tickets and journal entries. It confirmed everything second-hand knowledge had said: no allowlist at all, still the bare 07-03 version. I swept every pattern in the real private pattern file against `main`'s current tree rather than assume last month's list of hits was still the whole list, and found two I didn't know about — the pattern file also guards two OTHER private use cases nobody had mentioned to me tonight, and `DECISION_REGISTER.md` itself now tripped the gate, because recording DEC-12 necessarily meant writing the codename it was clearing. Zero hits for the other two use cases' patterns — nothing to worry about there — but it meant the allowlist had to be built to clear exactly five specific reviewed blobs, not to weaken the pattern itself.

He authorized the edit, then separately had to authorize running it, then separately had to authorize a browser navigation for a step I hadn't anticipated — three distinct permission prompts for three distinct actions, and a general "yes, do it yourself" in chat satisfied none of them ahead of time. That is worth remembering on its own: this harness's permission layer does not read intent from conversation, it gates each action class individually, and a broad verbal go-ahead is not a substitute for the specific one when the next blocked call arrives.

The push itself then failed for a reason that had nothing to do with any of tonight's actual work: GitHub's own server-side secret scanning blocked it, flagging a "live-shaped" Stripe key in `tests/integration/test_green_quality_security.py`. Reading the file settled it in under a minute — the docstring says outright that it is a planted, deliberately realistic fixture, built to prove BlarAI's own security scanner catches exactly this shape, with a second test asserting the value never leaks into that scanner's own output. Not a leak. But also not something this project's leak-gate could have caught on its own — `$builtinRegexes` checks for Anthropic, GitHub, and HuggingFace token shapes and nothing from Stripe. GitHub's scanner is a second, independent lock on the same door, and it closed exactly when the first one had a gap. That is the whole argument for defense-in-depth stated as a fact rather than a principle: two controls that check different things caught what one alone would have missed.

I could not clear GitHub's block myself the way I cleared my own — I don't have a login, and reshaping the fake key to dodge an unknown detector risked either not working or breaking the test whose entire job is looking realistic. GitHub provides the correct path for exactly this situation, a human review marking a specific flagged string as reviewed-and-safe, and that path needs a human. He drove it in his own browser in under a minute once I stopped trying to route around the permission layer and just said so plainly.

Both `blarai` and, on his direction, `agentic-setup` are now actually current on their public mirrors — not just bookkeeping-correct. `devplatform` / `jobhunt` / `jobhunt-eligibility` stayed untouched on his explicit instruction; I had zero context on any of them and had been about to run the same script against all three before the permission layer stopped me and I asked instead. That block was right to fire even though I did not immediately see why.

Gate/test status unaffected — no BlarAI test files changed in this arc, only `agentic-setup`'s publisher script and its allowlist.

**Proposed lesson:** *A fix that resolves the diagnosed cause is not the same as completing the task the diagnosis was in service of. "The ref was stale" was true and real; "therefore the mirror is fixed" did not follow, and only checking what the operator actually asked for — not what the investigation had made legible — closed the gap.*

**Next:** add a Stripe key-shape pattern to the local leak-gate's `$builtinRegexes` for defense in depth, so this class doesn't rely on GitHub's scanner alone next time (#1104). Deferred item 3 (fail-loud alerting) is still open.

### 2026-08-09 — Writing carefully about a guard can trip the guard

*Plain summary: A public README had been hand-patched with demo-film links and an update summary that never made it back into the source of truth, so the first real publish since that patch quietly deleted them. Restoring that content surfaced a smaller, sharper problem: my own careful, scoped write-ups of tonight's leak-gate work named the exact patterns they were describing, so publishing my own explanation tripped the thing being explained.*

The LA asked for a documented advancements summary on each public README. Before writing anything I checked what was actually live, because a comment from the 07-24 arc had already predicted the failure mode I was about to walk into: "the snapshot publishes local main's README verbatim each run… once the allowlist lands and the gate passes, the snapshot WILL clobber unless unified first." The allowlist landed tonight. The snapshot passed. Diffing the current public README against its last hand-patched commit confirmed it exactly — two embedded demo videos and a "Recent enhancements" section existed only on the public remote, never in local `main`, and my publish a few hours earlier had silently overwritten them with the plainer local version. Nothing was lost permanently; git keeps every snapshot's ancestry. But it would have happened again next week if I had only patched the symptom.

So the fix went into local `main` instead of the public remote directly: restored both films, and replaced the stale July section with a new one grounded in what's actually shipped since — the instrument-honesty arc, the acceptance-oracle fix, the first real operator review of a fleet-built site. That closes the exact gap the 07-24 comment named as deferred work, as a side effect of doing the actual ask properly rather than patching the public copy again.

Then both publishes failed on the leak-gate — for a reason with a shape worth keeping. `agentic-setup`'s own committed script failed on itself: the allowlist comment I wrote hours earlier, explaining precisely which patterns it was clearing and why, contained the literal pattern text and codenames it was describing. And `blarai` failed on my own journal entry from earlier tonight, where I had written out, by name, the two other private use cases the pattern file also guards — a sentence whose entire point was that I'd found and cleared *zero* real hits for them. Explaining a guard accurately and reproducing what the guard is looking for turned out to be the same act. Both were true, useful, honest sentences, and both had to be reworded — not deleted, not softened, just written without the literal trigger strings — because the alternative was to keep expanding an allowlist to cover my own commentary about the allowlist.

Gate unaffected — docs and one README-adjacent script comment only.

**Proposed lesson:** *A write-up that explains a content filter by naming exactly what it filters will itself match the filter, every time, because that is what "explains accurately" means. Describe the class and cite the ticket; let the ticket carry the specifics that would otherwise recreate the very pattern being described.*

**Next:** nothing outstanding on tonight's arc. `#1104`'s publisher-hardening half and `#1349`'s test fragility remain open for whoever picks them up.

### 2026-08-09 — A deadline is not a fault, and it took me eight tries to notice

*Plain summary: four nights of building produced nothing, and the reason was that a security check had three minutes to survive eight minutes of picture-making. I got the answer wrong seven times first, and the thing that finally settled it was a diagnostic I built that same afternoon and that immediately proved my latest theory wrong.*

The operator's builds stopped working on 7 August and nobody noticed for four nights, because the failed runs kept re-filing the *same* delivered website into a new dated folder every morning. The product went on existing while the pipeline that made it stopped working. That is the first thing worth writing down: an artifact that persists is not evidence that the thing which produced it still functions.

The message every failed run reported was `No response from the Assistant Orchestrator`. It is a lie, and it is a lie the codebase had already documented twice — `constants.py` says in prose that this sentence appears when *"the gateway's receive gave up while the AO was still generating"*. I read that file late. Before I did, I spent most of a day on seven hypotheses, and the shape of my error was identical every time: find a fault in a failing run, feel the click of recognition, and reach for the keyboard.

What kept saving me was one habit — go and read the run that WORKED. The crash in the image code? Present on the good night. The interactive chat window? An artifact of my own manual launch. The security handshake failing sixteen times? **Present on the good night too.** Each of those took a few minutes to check and each one cost me a theory I was already fond of.

And then that same habit produced the worst error of the day, because I applied it to the one thing it does not fit. I struck the handshake failure off the list on the grounds that the successful night had it as well. That reasoning is sound for a *fault* — a defect present during a success is not the cause of a failure. It is completely wrong for a **deadline**, where the question is never "did it fire" but "what finished before it did". The handshake fail-closes at 180 seconds on every single night. The good night generated two images in 98 seconds and beat it. The bad nights generate four, take 133 to 152 seconds, and get torn down mid-build. I had the right suspect in custody twice and released him on a test that could not see the crime.

The arithmetic, once I finally did it, is embarrassing in its simplicity. Generating one image costs 59.9 seconds — a figure measured on this hardware back in June and sitting in the performance directory the whole time. The pottery card declares eight assets. That is 479 seconds of graphics saturation inside a window that allowed 180. It could never have worked; it only ever *appeared* to work on the one night the plan happened to be small enough.

The fix is three constants and it is not clever. What I want to keep instead is the mechanism that produced the answer. Yesterday I built an instrument for a completely different problem — a stamp that read `not-run` on eighty-one records without ever saying why — and the lesson generalised: a fail-loud signal that cannot distinguish its own failure modes is why a defect goes a month unread rather than unnoticed. So when I found `No response` collapsing a timeout and a closed connection into one sentence, I fixed it the same way before doing anything else. **On its first live run it told me `connection closed after 133s, well inside the 900s budget` — and killed the root cause I had shipped four hours earlier.** I have rarely been so pleased to be wrong. The instrument cost twenty minutes and retired a theory I would otherwise have defended for a day.

Two smaller things are worth their own lines.

I raised a timeout and then had to raise a second constant I had not thought about, because the backoff cap and the budget together decide the *attempt count* — 600 seconds at a 15-second cap is forty-four attempts, and per-attempt time rides on top of the sleep budget, so the worst-case boot became twenty-two minutes. A number changed in isolation is a number changed carelessly.

And one test went red that I could have simply repinned. It asserted that every sleep after the fourth equals the cap — true only while the cap sat below the next doubling step, which my change ended. Repinning would have preserved a test that breaks on the next tuning change for no reason at all; it now asserts the rule rather than an accident of the old value. The difference between those two is most of what makes a suite worth having.

**Next:** the fix is in and a verification run is in flight. The honest position is that I have made the deadline survivable, not removed the contention — generation still happens inside a request-response window, and a site wanting twelve pictures will cross this budget too. That is written into the constant, the registry entry and its shrink condition, so the next person to read it inherits the limit along with the number.

### 2026-08-09 — The fix worked, and my explanation of why was wrong

*Plain summary: the builds run again after four lost nights. But when I checked which of my four changes actually did it, only one had — and the story I had written into the code comments, the ticket and the journal was about a different one. The run that proved me right also proved me wrong.*

Yesterday I wrote an entry about eight hypotheses and seven wrong ones, and closed it saying a verification run was in flight. It ran today at 14:03. It worked: the handshake succeeded, the Assistant Orchestrator survived, the dispatch was approved, and the coder fleet built for a full hour — the first finished dispatch work since the 7th. Two pages merged.

Then I did the thing I had spent all of yesterday learning to do, and checked the mechanism rather than the outcome.

I had changed four constants. The story I told about them — in the ticket, in the timeout registry, in three docstrings and in yesterday's journal entry — was that dispatch-asset generation saturates the graphics card for longer than the handshake's three-minute budget allows, so the budget had to grow. The arithmetic was clean and I was pleased with it. **It is not what fixed anything.**

The winning handshake took 16.35 seconds to negotiate. The old per-attempt ceiling was five. No quantity of five-second attempts could ever have completed that negotiation, and the old configuration made sixteen of them, each hitting the same wall, before giving up. The constant that mattered was the one bounding a single attempt — and I had described that change as a supporting detail while the aggregate budget took top billing. The budget raise did nothing: the handshake finished 113 seconds in, comfortably inside the old 180.

Worse, the contention story does not survive its own evidence. I went back to the failed night's log and read the timeline properly for the first time. The handshake began at 12:41:35 and had failed **nine times over 110 seconds before a single image was generated**. Asset generation started at 12:43:25, by which point the pattern was already established and unbroken. Graphics saturation lengthens the window in which the policy agent is unreachable; it does not open it. What I had diagnosed as a new contention class is the original cold-load class from #808, with a per-attempt ceiling too tight to clear it.

So: right fix, wrong reason, and the wrong reason was written into the surfaces a future session would read as settled fact. One of those surfaces was actively dangerous — the registry's review line said **"SHRINK back to ~5 s once dispatch-asset generation moves off the request thread,"** which is an instruction to re-break the system as a reward for fixing something else. I had written a trap and labelled it maintenance guidance.

What makes this worth an entry rather than a quiet correction is *why* the wrong story was so comfortable. It arrived at the end of a long day of being wrong, it explained the observed facts, and it came with arithmetic — 8 assets × 59.9 seconds = 479 seconds, longer than the budget, therefore the budget. Every step of that is true and the conclusion still does not follow, because a number that *could* have caused the failure is not evidence that it *did*. I never checked whether the failures started before the generation did. It took ninety seconds to check, once I thought to.

The per-image figure was wrong too, and in the direction that flattered my case: I had been quoting 59.9 seconds from a June probe of the interactive illustration path, which runs 30 denoising steps. The dispatch path runs 18. Measured on today's run: 303 seconds for 8 assets, 37.9 seconds each. My arithmetic overstated the requirement by 58%.

Two of the four constants earned their place. The per-attempt ceiling is the fix, measured. The EXECUTE budget was genuinely necessary and is now confirmed end-to-end — the approve request really did stay open for 303 seconds of generation, and the old default would have hung up at 14:09:22, mid-picture. The other two are unproven headroom and now say so in their own docstrings. Leaving all four looking equally load-bearing would have been exactly the instrument dishonesty this whole arc exists to retire, committed against my own work.

And then the run handed me one more thing. At 14:09:22 the boot log reads:

```
ERROR: send_prompt: connect failed — Fail-Closed
WARNING: stream_tokens: receive returned None — stream ended
INFO: get_pgov_result: not in cache (default deny)
INFO: Startup: Minimal prompt-flow preflight passed ✓
```

Eleven milliseconds between the failure and the tick. The preflight wrapped its round-trip in a try/except and returned success for anything that did not raise — and `send_prompt` deliberately does not raise on a failed connect, because the interactive path renders the empty stream itself. So zero tokens, an empty response and a default-denied governance lookup all sailed through as PASS. The comment above that function says it exists to catch a misrouted prompt at boot rather than at the user's first prompt. A misroute produces precisely that signature. **The gate could never have caught the defect it was built for, and reported a tick every boot while unable to.**

Fixing it needed two halves, and I want the pairing recorded because either alone is a defect. Asserting the round-trip is the actual fix. But asserting *without* retrying would fail the boot every time the box was busy — which is the four-lost-nights failure shape arriving from a new direction, a startup check tearing down a working assistant. The retry is also what makes the assertion diagnostic rather than merely strict: a misroute stays broken across attempts and contention clears, and a single attempt cannot tell them apart because both return nothing.

**Next:** the assistant is down for a clean gate and then goes straight back to work — the piece-detail page still has not been built, and today's run failed to reach it for a third distinct reason. Yesterday it was a torn-down orchestrator, before that a fake exam that deleted the task from the plan; today the page's dependency parked after the harness declared the run doomed while it was still, in fact, working. Three different causes, one page. That is what an integration surface looks like when you finally get to see it.

### 2026-08-09 — Three inferences, one log file, and a control I nearly broke for no reason

*Plain summary: I decided a safety mechanism was faulty and wrote a ticket telling a future session to weaken it. Then I read the log I should have read first, and the mechanism had been right all along — it had caught a real bug that has been quietly costing us deliveries since July.*

*(Proposed lesson: an inference that explains every observation is still not a measurement. Recurrence — see the same shape earlier today in the deadline-vs-fault entry.)*

Today's earlier entry was about getting a fix right for the wrong reason. This one is about the same failure arriving three more times in a single afternoon, and the third time it nearly cost something.

The battery ran and stopped short. The monitor had declared the job doomed and killed it, and the task it killed then finished normally six minutes later — which looks exactly like a watchdog with an itchy trigger. I wrote a ticket saying so. My explanation was that the liveness signals go dark during a candidate's verify-and-review phase, which is legitimate work the detector cannot see.

That was wrong, so I checked the file timestamps more carefully and produced a better wrong answer. The second-candidate log had been created at 14:52 and not touched again until 15:07, the model server's log stopped at 14:57, and nothing else moved. So — a single long generation, invisible to every witness at once, because the agent log is write-buffered and the processor probe reads idle on work the graphics card is doing. That story explained every timestamp I had. I wrote it up, called it the third instance of a recurring false alarm, noted that the window had already been widened once from ninety seconds to two hundred and forty, and concluded that widening it again could never converge.

It is a good story. I can still see why I believed it. And the part that should have stopped me is that I had *just* written an entry, hours earlier, about arithmetic that explained the observed facts without being what caused them.

What settled it was measuring the thing I had asserted. I sampled the model server's processor use during a live generation on the next run: fourteen, seventeen, twenty-four, twenty-nine percent — against a threshold of five. The probe is not blind to graphics-bound work at all. My mechanism was refuted by the first measurement anyone took of it.

So I finally opened the candidate's log and read the end of it. Two lines:

```
21:57:27Z  evaluated permission=bash pattern="node src/server.js" action=allow
22:07:28Z  cancel session — error=Aborted
```

The coder had started a web server and waited for it to exit. Web servers do not exit. It sat there for ten minutes and one second until something outside killed it. Nothing was generating. Nothing was reviewing. **The detector was right, the task was genuinely dead, and the stop it issued is the only reason the run did not sit there until the four-hour ceiling.**

I had written a ticket instructing a future session to weaken a control that was working — the second time in one day I authored that exact kind of trap, after removing one from the timeout registry that morning telling someone to shrink a five-second ceiling back down.

Then the finding got better, because a bug that hangs a coder for ten minutes should not be a one-off. I searched all 932 banked agent logs for commands that start servers. Seventeen have one. Measuring the gap between that command and the log's last line:

```
602s  aborted   battery-b9-pottery-site create-piece-list-page   2026-08-09
601s  aborted   battery-b5-habit-web    create-habit-tracking    2026-08-06
553s            authorwebsite3000       create-header-component  2026-07-27
519s            battery-b5-habit-web    create-habit-tracking    2026-07-14
330s            battery-b7-util-trio    password-generator-help  2026-07-12
276s  aborted   battery-b7-util-trio    slugify-phrase-helper    2026-07-12
```

Six different cards, four weeks, and the same shape every time. And the second row is the run I had cited *in my own ticket* as the previous instance of a false alarm. It was not a false alarm. It was this bug, and that detector was right too. Two of the three cases I had inherited and repeated as watchdog misfires were real hangs nobody had opened the log for.

The bug is worth its own line: a coder building a web application will, sooner or later, try to start the web application. The permission layer evaluated `node src/server.js`, allowed it, and blocked. The coder runtime has no per-command timeout — I checked its published configuration schema, and there is nothing between provider timeouts and message-protocol timeouts that bounds a shell command. What the configuration does have is a permission map and a flag that returns a denied command to the model instead of killing the task, so the fix is to refuse the server-start shapes and let the coder pick something bounded. That is a smaller fix than it deserves, and I have written down that it is smaller than it deserves rather than letting the deny-list read as a solved problem.

> **Correction, 2026-08-10 06:20.** *"The coder runtime has no per-command timeout — I checked its published configuration schema"* is **false**, and the way it is false is the point. `~/.config/opencode/plugin/command-timeout.js` caps bash at 300 s with a 10 s server probe, has been live since 2026-07-10, and prints its banner in 771 of 969 banked agent logs. The schema check was accurate and the inference from it was not: a published schema describes what a product's *config* accepts, and a plugin is a different mechanism the schema knows nothing about. I verified against a description of the product while the running system sat one search away on the same machine — which is the documentation-is-not-evidence rule, committed in an entry about being wrong for plausible reasons. Worse: the plugin was written for this exact command (its comment names `node src/server.js`, from incident #688), I then assumed its pattern must miss the bare form, tested it, and found it **matches**. So the true finding is that a 10-second cap was loaded, matched the command, and did not bound a 602-second hang — a control shipped and trusted for a month that does not hold, with the reason still unverified. The deny-list stands, for a better reason than the entry gives: not a substitute for a missing bound, but a second lock on a door whose first lock is measurably failing. #1357.

The thing I want to keep is not the bug. It is that three consecutive explanations survived every piece of evidence I had gathered, and all three were wrong, and the file that answered it in thirty seconds had been sitting on disk the entire time. Timestamps tell you when something was written. They cannot tell you what it says. I keep reaching for the cheap signal and building a mechanism out of it, and the mechanism keeps being plausible, and plausible keeps being enough to make me stop looking.

There is a small mercy in how this surfaced. The harness has a guard, built last week, that shouts when a scorecard is written while a driver is still live — *"this card records the end of OBSERVATION, not the end of the run."* It fired correctly and it is the reason any of this was legible instead of being banked as an ordinary stall. An instrument built to make a specific confusion impossible did its job, on a day when the person reading it made three different confusions anyway.

A postscript, forty minutes later, which is the reason this entry exists rather than the ticket alone. I built a watcher to catch the hang on the next run, and its rule was: an agent log that has been quiet for more than 150 seconds after a shell call is hung. It fired almost immediately, on a candidate that had run `node tests/structure.test.js` — a test, which terminates — and then written `exiting loop` and `disposing instance` and stopped, because it had **finished**.

I had encoded *quiet means stuck* into my own instrument, within the hour, having just spent an afternoon proving that the watchdog which allegedly made that mistake had not made it. The fix is one line — a finished candidate leaves a terminal marker, so require its absence — but the point is that knowing the fallacy by name did not stop me from building it. It is not a knowledge problem. The cheap signal is right there, it correlates with the thing you want, and it takes a deliberate act to ask what else produces the same signal.

**Next:** the deny-list patch is staged and held — the coder harness loads from the live tree and a battery is running, so it lands when the run does. The residuals are on the ticket, including the honest one: a blocklist inside an allow-by-default permission map is not the principled answer, and I am not going to let it read as one.

### 2026-08-09 — An eight-hour window, three hours of run, and no record of the swap

*Plain summary: a build was given eight hours and killed after three. The system announced the eight hours in its own log and then used a different number, and nothing anywhere wrote down which one it had picked. I could not work out why, so I stopped and built the thing that will tell me, rather than shipping the fix that felt right.*

*(Proposed lesson: a fallback that cannot say why it fell back is not fail-safe, it is fail-silent. Sibling to the deadline-vs-fault entry from this morning.)*

The third B9 run of the day got further than any before it. The home page merged. The piece-list page — the exact task that hung on a web server earlier and cascaded into the operator's individual-piece page being skipped — merged cleanly this time. Then, four tasks in, the run was killed mid-task and the whole thing was recorded as a harness stall.

The card had been granted a 28,800-second envelope. The battery said so in its own log, plainly. The run died at 10,901 seconds, which is the 10,800-second campaign default plus the time it takes to put the model back. Over five hours of the card's own budget went unspent, and the wave containing the missing page was never started.

What the run had to say about this, in full:

```
The overall run budget elapsed — restoring the 14B.
```

That sentence is exactly as true of a three-hour budget as of an eight-hour one. It identifies nothing. And when I went looking, both ends of the channel that carries the number turned out to be mute in the same way: the writer swallowed every filesystem error and returned nothing, and the reader returned a bare "no" for absent, unreadable, corrupt, cleared and expired alike, while the caller fell back to the default without logging a word. Four different faults, one indistinguishable signature. The defect was not undiagnosed; it was **undiagnosable**, and would have stayed that way through any number of repeat runs.

So I did the eliminations that evidence supports. The ceiling is 28,800 exactly, so nothing was clamped. Both code trees resolve the file to the same path — I ran the resolution from each of them rather than assuming. Exactly one function in the codebase reads it, so nothing consumed it first. The one branch that could skip the write needs a missing config, and that field is required by the dataclass, so it cannot happen in a battery run.

That leaves expiry: the staged file is ignored after five minutes, and between staging and the moment it is read sits the entire planning phase — a phase we deliberately budget **twelve** minutes for, because a cold plan legitimately takes that long. A five-minute guard inside a twelve-minute preamble is a plausible mismatch.

Plausible. Which today is a word I have learned to distrust.

I had already written that the timeline cleared expiry — staged at 15:41:47, approved at 15:43:57, a comfortable 131 seconds. Then I checked where 15:41:47 had come from, and it was a *directory* timestamp. A directory's timestamp records entries appearing, not files being written. It does not date the thing I used it to date. My elimination was worth nothing, and I had already published it.

That is the third time in one day I built a mechanism out of the cheapest available signal and got it wrong. The first cost me a wrong root cause. The second cost me a ticket telling a future session to weaken a control that was working. This one only cost a paragraph, because I checked it before it hardened into a fix.

And that is the part worth keeping. The obvious move was to widen the five-minute window — one constant, plausible story, and the next run probably succeeds. I didn't, and I want the reasoning on the record: twice today a theory that explained every observation turned out to be false, and loosening a guard around a fault you have not identified is how the *next* mystery gets built. If the window is innocent, I would have widened it for nothing and buried the real cause under a change that looked like a fix.

What shipped instead is the instrument. Every path now names itself with its arithmetic — *rejected as stale: 28,800 s written N seconds ago, window 300 s*; *absent: <the actual path it looked at>*; *clamped: the card asked for X*; *honored*. The writer returns a real answer and the battery shouts if a stage fails to land. Eleven tests, including a toggle proof that the writer reports failure rather than swallowing it, and an end-to-end check on 28,800 exactly, since it sits *on* the ceiling and is the value most likely to be quietly mishandled by an off-by-one.

Tonight's run reads that line out loud. Whichever of the three it prints names the fix, and I would rather spend one night learning the answer than three nights re-running a guess.

One smaller thing, since it cost two full gate runs. I edited source files while the test suite was running, twice. Python's `inspect.getsource` maps a loaded function's line numbers against the file **as it is on disk right now** — so shifting a file mid-run makes source-asserting tests read back a completely different function and fail for reasons that have nothing to do with them. The first time I nearly filed it as a defect. The gate's own exit code said `0` while the summary line said three had failed, which is the same trap already written down for pipes and which I walked into anyway. *Do not touch the tree while the gate runs* is now in the doctrine snapshot, where it should have been already.

**Next:** the 23:00 battery runs the same card with all four of today's fixes and a log line that finally states which budget it chose. The individual-piece page has now been blocked by three distinct causes on three consecutive runs — a fake exam, a hung web server, and a budget nobody wrote down. Each one was invisible until an instrument was built for it, and none of them was the thing anyone suspected first.


### 2026-08-10 — A control can be present, loaded, matched, and inert

*Plain summary: the seven-page site finally got built; the ten-second safety cap that was supposed to stop yesterday's ten-minute hang had been working perfectly all along and could never have helped, because a single `&` at the end of the command made it race the wrong thing.*

The page exists. `create-piece-detail-page` had been eaten by six distinct causes since 2026-08-07, and last night's run delivered all seven tasks on the first dispatch each — home, contact, piece list, header menu, styles, the individual-piece page at 5034 bytes, and the acceptance tests. First complete B9 delivery. I want to be precise about what that means, because the job-level exam was refused as defective before it ran, so "delivered" means the tasks merged, not that an independent check said the site was good.

What I came back to was the question I refused to guess at yesterday. The coder had run `node src/server.js` and blocked for 602 seconds. A plugin caps every bash command at five minutes and gives server-shaped commands a ten-second probe instead. Yesterday I established three things and stopped: the plugin was loaded, its pattern matched the command, and the command still hung. I wrote *"why is UNVERIFIED and is not guessed at here"* and went to bed, having already guessed wrong twice on that ticket.

I started in the shipped binary rather than the documentation, since documentation is what got me last time. Inside a 165 MB compiled bundle I found `Optional timeout in seconds` and felt the click of a units bug — the plugin passes milliseconds, so a ten-second cap would have become two and a half hours. It was the wrong string. It belongs to `webfetch`. The bash tool three megabytes away says milliseconds, and its sleep is written `millis`. That one nearly went into a ticket.

The answer was in the run's own record, in a field I had never looked at. Every tool call the coder makes is journalled with its arguments, and the arguments for that command read `"timeout": 10000`. The plugin had fired. The clamp had landed. Every link I had spent two days doubting was working. And in the same line was the command, which is not what I had written on the ticket — it ends with an ampersand:

```
cd C:/Users/.../battery-b9-pottery-site-create-piece-list-page && node src/server.js &
```

The tool's timeout races three things: the child exiting, an external abort, and a sleep for the timeout duration. Whichever wins decides whether the process is killed. Then, *after* the race, outside every one of its branches, it reads the command's output to end-of-stream. With a trailing `&` the shell forks the server and exits in about a second, so the race resolves as `exit` — no kill branch runs, the ten-second sleep loses by winning nothing, and the tool sits down to read a pipe that a detached server is still holding open. The timeout bounds a command that runs too long. `&` turns it into a command that finishes immediately and leaves a pipe open.

I tested it standalone and got the answer wrong on the first try. `timeout 20 bash -c 'node srv.js &' > out.txt` returned in one second, and I wrote "hypothesis refuted" in my head. Then I noticed I had redirected to a *file*. Piped — which is what opencode does — the same command never returned at all; it blew past the twenty-second timeout, past two minutes, and had to be killed. The pipe is the entire mechanism, and I had built an experiment that carefully excluded it.

So the honest statement is stronger than "a bound existed and did not hold." The bound was correctly applied and is structurally incapable of covering this shape. That is worth more than the fix: **a control can be present, loaded, matched, and inert.** Three of capability 8's failure paths were each read as a missing control when the control was there. The question worth asking of a guard is not whether it exists but what it races, and whether the thing you fear can win that race by finishing early.

The obvious fix was to lower the probe cap, and I want the rejection on the record: it would not have saved one second. Ten seconds or ten milliseconds, the shell still exits first. What shipped instead refuses the shape — a trailing `&` is rewritten to a message telling the coder to import the module and call it from a test, the same treatment the browser-open rule already gets. It also closes a real gap in the pattern, `node app.js`, which matched nothing. Twelve tests pass; with the new branch deleted, exactly the two detach tests fail and the false-positive test still passes, so it is catching the incident rather than asserting its own existence. Last time I wrote a toggle proof its escaping was broken and it disabled nothing while passing.

Two things I had never checked about the deny-list I shipped the night before turned out to matter. opencode resolves permissions with `findLast` — the *last* matching rule wins — and my twenty-three denials sit at indices 15 through 37, below the `"*": "allow"` wildcard at index 0. Had I appended them above it, the entire control would have been silently inert, which is the same sentence as the paragraph above and a much less forgivable version of it. It is correct by authoring accident. And opencode evaluates each parsed sub-command separately, so `cd … && node src/server.js` is judged as `node src/server.js` and the prefix defeats nothing.

Then the run gave me the observable I had asked for and said I would not accept a weaker version of. At 14:25:26 this morning: `pattern="node src/server.js"`, `action=deny`. The coder was told a rule prevented the call, and three minutes later ran `node --test tests/page-structure.test.js` instead. Not the absence of a hang — a refusal recovered from. Yesterday I wrote that absence would prove nothing, and I am glad I did, because the proof that arrived is a different and better thing. In the same log the coder started a listening server anyway, by inlining it in `node -e "… server.listen(3000, …)"`, and was allowed. Not an evasion attempt; just the same intent in a shape the blocklist does not enumerate. The weakness I described as theoretical at 18:03 had a measured instance by 14:15 the next day.

The other finding came from scoring the run rather than from any gate. The visual check renders `public/index.html`. That file is 1033 bytes with a modification time equal to the scaffold's own creation, untouched by all seven tasks — `<h1>App</h1>`, a blue circle with the letter A. The site that was built lives in six other files. So the design reviewer's verdict, *"blank/unrendered … no indication of any of the required pages,"* was exactly right about the page it was shown and silent about the product, and the executability floor recorded `pass :: served + loaded + zero console errors`, which is also true of a placeholder. Worse, `src/server.js` serves `/` from `index.html`, so the operator's front door was the placeholder, and the built pages disagreed about where home was: the shared menu pointed at `home.html`, three pages pointed back at the stub.

Three surfaces each behaved reasonably. The seed brief tells the coder its first edit should be `public/index.html`. The decomposer asked for a task called `create-home-page` and never named a file. The capture walks a fixed list whose first entry always exists in this scaffold. Nothing reconciles them, and a task can be completed, merged, wave-gated and design-reviewed while the front door is untouched.

I filed that at 07:52 and included a prediction dressed as an observation: that the design-fix dispatch was spending an eighth dispatch on a page it could not fix. At 08:14 I looked in the worktree. `index.html` was 3221 bytes, rewritten at 08:00 into a real home page with the shared header and navigation pointing at the pages that exist. The visual loop was the only control in the entire run that noticed the product had no front door, and it repaired it. It got there blind — never told six pages existed, it authored a seventh home page rather than wiring the entry point — so the residual is duplication rather than breakage, and far smaller than what it solved. I had described a run I had not read, twenty-two minutes before reading it, and the description sounded like a measurement.

That is the same failure as the ticket it was filed on, one level up, on a day whose whole subject was the difference between a control that is there and a control that works. Both corrections went in dated rather than scrubbed: `9dae4fdd` for the root cause, `a7c65a67` for the retraction. The journal keeps what I believed at the time because the gap between 07:52 and 08:14 is the only part of this worth teaching.

> **CORRECTION, 08:50, written after the run ended.** The retraction above is *also* wrong, and it is the one that mattered. The 3221-byte page never shipped. `public/index.html` on the delivered site is **1044 bytes**, and the only commit that ever touched it after the seed changed `<title>App</title>` to `<title>Pottery Studio</title>`. Nothing else. The run's own log says why, three times: *"visual FIX #N not applied: coder timed out on the FIX pass; kept the prior merged version."* The work was not rejected by a gate — each fix pass hit a time bound and its output was discarded, and then the worktree was disposed with the changes uncommitted. The project repo has one branch and no parked work. The page is gone and the operator's front door is still the placeholder.
>
> The design-fix task ran ninety-seven minutes and merged a title. The vision model's own verdict is the proof the page existed: it moved from *"blank/unrendered … a single blue circle with the letter 'A'"* to *"the menu items 'Home', 'Gallery', 'Contact' are not aligned with the logo … the 'About Our Studio' heading is visually disconnected."* You cannot critique alignment on a placeholder.
>
> The asymmetry is the defect, and it is filed as #1370: on the same run, the same task, a bounded **base** candidate got *"work kept, the gate decides the merge"* and merged; a bounded **fix** pass got its work deleted with no gate consulted. One path judges what a bounded agent produced; the other assumes it produced nothing. And the summary the operator reads says `RESULT: MERGED into your project - just open the app and try it.`
>
> Three readings of one dispatch, each confidently wrong, each corrected by the next measurement — while writing an entry about not trusting the cheapest available signal. The first two were cheap to fix. This one cost the deliverable.

**Next:** the plugin fix and its twelve tests are written and green but cannot land while a dispatch is live in that tree, so they merge when the run drains, along with the correction branch that has been waiting on a full gate for the same reason. The open thread is no longer the hang — it is that the visual loop is currently the safety net for a gap no deterministic gate covers, and that it is doing that job without being told what was built.

### 2026-08-10 — A file that exists is not a file that is served

*Plain summary: he asked to be handed the website served and running, opened it, and found the gallery empty — the data file was sitting one directory outside the folder the server publishes, and not one of our four checks had ever looked at that page.*

He said it plainly: *"insofar as the pottery website evaluation and validation, i will of course need it handed to me served and running. that should be standard. i am non-technical."*

The uncomfortable part is that it already was standard. `Review Website.cmd` has sat at the root of the coding repo for days — it copies a build somewhere safe, serves it, opens it, and walks him through his own goal one sentence at a time with "I couldn't tell" as a real answer. It is a good tool. **It had simply stopped firing.** Every review snapshot on the box was dated 08-08, so the site built overnight sat unopened, which is the exact failure it was written to end. The copy still running on port 8420 was two days old; anyone who had looked at "the site" recently was looking at the wrong one.

So I served last night's build by hand and gave him the address. Then he opened the gallery and sent me a screenshot with three words in red: **Failed to load pieces.**

Both of the pages his goal is actually about — the list of pieces for sale, and the individual piece page — run `fetch('./data/pieces.json')`. The file exists. Six pieces, real content, 841 bytes. But the delivered server publishes exactly one directory, `public/`, and `data/` sits *beside* it rather than inside. Every visitor gets a 404. His words for that requirement were *"the list of pieces and each piece's own page have to come from the same saved information"* — which cannot hold at runtime as built, not because the shared data is missing, but because it is unreachable.

His actual question was better than the bug: shouldn't the vision model have noticed? I counted. The string `pieces.html` appears **zero** times in the run's visual-capture log and **zero** times in the design-critique log. The model never saw the page. Every visual and console instrument opens `index.html` and stops, which is the placeholder defect I had filed that morning and priced far too cheaply.

Four instruments, four separate ways of missing the same thing. The console check reported "zero console errors" — true of the page it loaded, which was not this one. The link checker resolves `href` and `src`; a `fetch` in JavaScript is neither, so it is structurally blind. And the image checker *did* read `data/pieces.json` — off the filesystem — and graded the pictures listed inside it, finding that three products share one photo of a mug. It never asked whether a browser could reach the file it was reading.

That last one is the sentence worth keeping. **Every instrument that touched this data read it from the filesystem; nothing ever fetched it the way a visitor does. A file that exists is not a file that is served.**

The fix went into `link_lint` rather than a new module, because that surface already owns served-root detection, path resolution, severity grading and the wiring — a parallel checker would have duplicated all four. It now resolves `fetch()` targets, and on the real delivered tree it returns two HIGH findings and `hard: True`.

It is deliberately narrow, and the reason is a comment the original author left. `extract_references_js` excluded `fetch()` on purpose: *"a broader sweep would flag fetch() calls against API routes, which are not files and are not defects."* That reasoning is correct and I kept it exactly — a fetch target is checked only when it names a *file*, something carrying a recognised extension. `fetch('/api/pieces')` is still ignored, and there is a test locking that. The exclusion was never wrong; it was one clause too broad, and the missing clause is the one that separates a file from a route. Extending someone's rule by finding the case they did not have is a better outcome than deciding they were careless.

Then I went to build what he asked for — the nightly battery serving the site it built and leaving it running — and nearly broke tonight's run.

The obvious code is `$r = & review-website.ps1 …`: call it, read the port out of what it returns. I had actually run that by hand at 09:42 to serve his copy. At 10:18 I went looking for the result and the call was **still blocked**, thirty-six minutes later, while the site served happily on 8421. The script had done its job and left a node server running, exactly as designed; the server inherited the parent's stdout, and reading that pipe to end-of-stream never returns.

That is the same mechanism, to the letter, that let `node src/server.js &` hang a coder for 602 seconds — the defect I had spent the morning proving and shipping a fix for. It found me again before lunch, in my own tooling, in the feature written to close it. Had I shipped the obvious version it would have hung in the night's postlude *before* the assistant teardown, leaving the 14B resident in memory through his entire morning.

So the night launches it detached, redirects to a file rather than a pipe — the file case terminates, the pipe case does not, measured both ways this morning — and reads the port from an artifact the tool already writes, because a detached process has no return value to read. Verified end to end: extracted 8421 from the file, fetched a page, 200. The wait is bounded at 180 seconds against a measured ~90, and a timeout is reported in his morning report rather than swallowed, because "no address appeared" has to read as a fault and not as "no site".

One more thing I got caught doing. The new tests pinned their evidence to `C:/Users/mrbla/projects/…`, and the first full gate came back red: a control refusing tests that point at a tree the fleet rewrites nightly. That rule is in my standing instructions in those words. It suggested retargeting to the night archives, which does not work here — the archive holds the tree from *before* the run that built these pages, so the delivery exists in no archive at all. The right home turned out to be the review workspace: the tool copies a build to a dated directory precisely so the reviewed bytes cannot change under the reviewer, which makes it the immutable record of what he was looking at. The same gate run also showed seven skips, and those were mine too — I had left the assistant running. Down, the tree is zero skipped.

The trade I want on the record: he deferred to me on whether a run should *refuse* to report success until the site is served. I built the reporting form, not the refusing one. A control that can block the night should not be handed the power to fail one on the same day I discovered its serving path can hang for thirty-six minutes. It reports now; it can gate once it has run unattended a few times and earned it.

**Next:** tonight is the first night that ends with a site he can open and an address in the morning report. The root under both of today's defects is still open — every instrument looks at `index.html` and calls it the product — and until the capture resolves the page a visitor actually reaches, the next broken page will be found the same way this one was: by him, opening it.

### 2026-08-10 — You find an inert control by counting what it did, not by reading what it says

*Plain summary: five separate guardrails turned out to have never once changed an outcome, and every one of them was found the same way — by counting events in the logs rather than by reading the code that was supposed to be protecting us.*

This morning's entry ended on a control that was present, loaded, matched, and inert. I thought that was the day's finding. It was the day's *shape*, and by evening I had five of them.

The operator asked to be handed the website served and running. Doing that surfaced a dead gallery, and chasing the dead gallery surfaced a fetch nothing could resolve, and building the check for that surfaced a front door that reached nothing, and none of that is what this entry is about. What it is about is that after the fourth one I stopped reading code first.

Here is the pattern, four times.

**The visual-fix loop.** Last night three fix passes were discarded with *"coder timed out on the FIX pass."* I filed it as an incident and wrote the four-line patch. Then I counted, across two runs: **35 fix cycles, 11 applied, 24 discarded, and every one of the 24 was that same branch.** Not one was rejected on merit — no conflict, no failed verify, no empty coder. About sixty-nine percent of every visual improvement this system has ever produced was deleted before anything looked at it, and `create-home-page` lost three of three on both runs, which is precisely why the operator's placeholder front door survived a whole night of a loop explicitly trying to fix it. The ticket stopped being an incident report and became a rate.

**The requirement-coverage instrument.** Capability 17 has read "no run has ever stamped it" for weeks. I filed that this morning as the instrument not being wired into the path a real run takes. It is wired. It runs. Its gate skips the coverage map whenever the exam has a hard finding, and "this exam is too thin" counts as hard — which every B9 run reports. So it was skipped on every run in the project's history, and the artifact honestly said *not measured*, and I read that as absence. The guard was right for exams that cannot be parsed and wrong for exams that are merely thin, which is exactly the case where *which requirement did nobody assert?* is the question worth asking.

**The permission map.** While gathering command statistics for an unrelated posture decision I counted `asking` events: **269 raised, zero replied, and 256 of 256 gated commands completed anyway.** In a headless dispatch nobody answers a permission prompt, so `ask` is not a weaker `deny` — it is `allow` with a control's name on it. Eleven rules, including every credential-file pattern, have never once altered an outcome. I had written the mechanism down myself the previous day, as the rationale for choosing `deny` over `ask` for twenty-three new rules, and never turned around to look at the rules already sitting there.

**And the one I'd have got wrong.** The obvious fix for the permission map is to flip the whole thing to an allowlist — deny by default, which is the project's own principle 2. So I measured the coder's actual vocabulary across a thousand logs first: 4,193 commands, and **`python -m` plus `python -c` plus `node -e` are fifty-four percent of everything it does.** Those are interpreters. An allowlist must admit them or the coder cannot work, and the moment it does it permits everything any denied command could have expressed differently. Command-shape filtering cannot bound this agent in either direction. I changed my recommendation from *probably flip it* to *keep it, stop calling it a security boundary, and put the effort into the sandbox instead* — and the thing that changed my mind was a percentage, not an argument.

The common thread is not that these were bad controls. Three of the four are well-reasoned code whose author's stated intent I agree with. The thread is that **"present in the configuration" and "operating" are different facts, and only one of them is measurable.** You cannot read a control and learn whether it fires; the code says what it would do, and every one of these would have done the right thing under the conditions its author was imagining. What tells you is a count: how many times did this branch execute, how many replies did this prompt receive, how many artifacts did this stage produce.

Every one of today's findings was a division. Thirty-five fix cycles into twenty-four discards. Two hundred and sixty-nine asks into zero replies. A thousand logs into zero coverage artifacts. None of them needed a debugger and none of them took more than a few minutes once I stopped reading and started counting.

There is a smaller lesson underneath, and it cost me an assertion. `verify-critique-loop.ps1` contained a test asserting *"coder timeout → commit is NOT attempted (no partial work)."* That is a test encoding the defect, with an intentional-sounding name for deletion, and it would have failed my fix and made the fix look wrong. I rewrote it as three tests rather than deleting it so the reversal stays legible. This is the same shape as the three tests pinned to the old doom window that kept a wrong value for a month: a test pinned to a behaviour argues against fixing that behaviour, and it argues in the voice of the suite.

One piece of good news worth recording beside all this, because the contrast is the point. BlarAI's own consent surfaces — egress and image generation — fail **closed**, and not by docstring: no verifier registered returns `False` in code, and both paths break out of the tool loop on any non-approval. The product was written to the principle. The workshop was configured to it, and a configuration expresses an intent the runtime is free to ignore. That is the two-rulebook split failing in a direction I had not seen named — not hobbling the workshop with the product's rules, but assuming it already had them.

I also spent two hours today being confidently wrong about a slowdown. Tasks were taking seventy-odd minutes against last night's forty, I had run a test sweep against the box, and I was about to bank *my own mistake cost the run time* as measured fact. It isn't: tasks two and three ran hours after the sweep died and were just as slow. The measured difference is that the battery runs BelowNormal so the operator's machine stays usable, and overnight there is nothing to preempt it. I did not touch that setting — it is the trade the daytime posture exists to make, and it is his.

**Next:** five branches are built and green and unmerged, waiting for a box quiet enough to gate honestly, which is the right order and the one I inverted earlier today. The counting habit is worth keeping pointed at the remaining controls rather than only at the ones an incident happened to surface — the useful question for each is not *does this look right* but *how many times has it changed an outcome, and can I show the number?*

### 2026-08-11 — The night ran, and every instrument told me it hadn't

*Plain summary: a battery night failed three different ways in one evening, and each time the thing that was supposed to notice was itself downstream of the failure. The fixes are two watchdogs that live outside the night — one of which caught two bugs in itself before it was trusted.*

He asked a simple question: how did last night go, and are we ready for tonight. The honest answer took four hours to assemble and was wrong twice on the way.

**The first wrong answer.** Last night's run left a 0-byte runner log, empty scorecards, and a tail full of `no opencl gpu device is available`. I read that as a GPU failure seven minutes in and told him so. Both halves were wrong. Those OpenCL lines appear twice in every *successful* night — routine oneDNN device-probe noise. And the runner is a blocking call whose output is buffered; a good night emits only 2–5 KB, under the flush threshold, so the log exists at all only because the process *exits*. Kill it and you get exactly 0 bytes regardless of how long it worked. The night had in fact run 7.5 productive hours and produced twelve commits before an unclean shutdown at 09:27 killed it an hour short of its budget. What caught both errors was one comparison against a night known to have succeeded. **A single night's logs cannot distinguish its own noise from its own failure** — neither error was visible from the failed night alone.

**The second.** At the start of the session I went looking for exactly the failure that was about to happen: could the timezone move (Pacific → Eastern, +3h) suppress tonight's fire? I grepped the battery's bootstrap for a date-keyed "already ran today" guard, found none, and reported it clear. The guard was real. It lives in Windows Task Scheduler, one layer above where I looked. The +3h shift re-dated last night's 23:00 Pacific run as 02:00 Eastern *today*, the daily trigger saw the day already satisfied, and it advanced to tomorrow. I proved a mechanism absent from the component I inspected and reported it absent from the system. The value was on my screen in my first command — `NextRun` read `8/11 11:00 PM` then and `8/12` later — and I re-read the wall clock all night while never re-reading the thing I had made a claim about.

**The third** was the interesting one. Started by hand at 23:08, the night stood down after four minutes: the archive guard refused to touch the sandbox because last night's power-killed run still "owned" it. That guard is correct and exists because a night once destroyed a live build. But its escape hatch — the swap driver's `14B is back` marker — requires the dead run to have survived long enough to write its own sign-off. **A run that was switched off cannot.** So the block was permanent: every future night, four minutes after launch, until a human intervened. The code's own comments name this class (#1360); the mitigation simply cannot reach the power-loss case it most needs to cover.

Reaping it posed the night's sharpest small choice. The guard would have been satisfied by one line appended to last night's log saying the run finished cleanly. Fast, and a lie written into the evidence record to unblock a queue. I renamed the journal to retire it instead, left every byte intact, and dropped a file recording the proof and the undo. *Retiring a record* is true; *the run finished cleanly* is not. Proof of death came from something the corpse could not fake: the machine had rebooted since its last write. That predicate — boot time after last write — is what #1380 proposes the guard should use, because every existing escape asks the dead run to sign its own death certificate.

**The pattern under all three:** every instrument in this system reports from *inside* the thing being measured. The morning report is written by the night, in its postlude. Kill the night and it writes nothing, so the operator's morning read shows the previous night and looks normal. Silence and success are the same observable. He found this himself with one question — "do you have a dead man's watch?" — which was a better diagnosis than anything I had produced to that point.

So: two watchdogs, both outside every git repo, both scheduled independently of the night. One asks at 23:30 whether a night *started*; one asks at 09:00 whether it *finished*, and rewrites the morning report itself when it didn't — the alarm lands on the file he already opens, and replaces the stale one so it cannot be misread.

**And the watchdog caught two bugs in itself.** First test-fire declared a healthy running night dead: its liveness probe recognised only the final stage, so preflight looked like death. Fixed, then tested the *other* direction and found worse — the probe matched any process whose command line merely *contained* the battery's script names, and the thing it matched was my own command, which had just written those words into its own arguments. That one is a false **negative**: it makes the watchdog go quiet, and quiet is indistinguishable from healthy. A watchdog anyone can mute by mentioning it is not a watchdog. I had already reported it to him as built, twice.

The correction I most want to keep: I then wrote that this made every process check in the codebase suspect. I swept it, and that is false — `run-battery-night.ps1:746` anchors on `-File`/`-m`, and every Python site matches on process *name*. Zero unanchored predicates in production. All three naive checks were mine, written in this session, hours after I had read the correctly-anchored reference implementation. **The pattern was not missing; I read that file for the answer to my question and walked past the technique it was demonstrating.** Which is the same shape as the other two errors — taking the nearest evidence as sufficient without asking what would actually settle it.

Cost: one night lost entirely, tonight delayed 17 minutes, and a permanent blocker that would have silently eaten every subsequent night. Banked: #1378, #1379, #1380 (top priority, fix specified), two armed watchdogs proven in both directions, and a timezone bug in the Claude config that had my two shells reporting times three hours apart — which, had I read the clock through the wrong one, would have had me telling him the battery was three hours away when it was sixteen minutes.

*(Folded 2026-08-14, three days late, from a draft held outside the repo because the primary checkout is the system under measurement during a battery night. The delay is itself evidence: the fold was owed "on the next quiet tree", and the tree was quiet the whole time because the battery was wedged. Lesson 349 minted; the second error is a fresh instance of lesson 297 and the watchdog's two self-bugs are the 222 family. Every "Next" below was still outstanding when this was folded — see the 2026-08-14 entry, where the blocker this entry predicted went on to eat five nights, exactly as written.)*

**Next:** land the #1380 boot-time predicate so the next power cut doesn't wedge the battery; move both watchdogs into the repo with tests; write the LESSONS entry on evidence sufficiency and the FIELD_NOTES line on self-referential process matching.

### 2026-08-14 — Five nights of nothing, and the guard that was blocking the only thing that could clear it

*Plain summary: the nightly coder had banked no measurement since the 10th. One guard, doing exactly what it was written to do, had wedged itself shut in a way that could only be opened from outside — and the ticket describing the fix had been filed three days earlier and not built.*

He came back after four days away and asked me to get the effort back on track. The first honest thing to say is that the effort had not been *slowed*; it had been stopped, deterministically, since the 12th, and every instrument that could have told him so had told him the truth in a form nobody was there to read.

The battery — the thing that runs the coder against a real website goal each night — archives last night's sandbox before it starts. Before archiving it asks whether a previous run still owns that folder, because a night once destroyed a live build by assuming the box was idle. It answers by counting `TASK-START` against `TASK-END` in the run's journal. More starts than ends means somebody is still building.
