The trade-off I made deliberately: I did not upgrade anything. Everything here argues for
moving to 2026.3.1, and I could have prepared the lock file tonight. But a runtime
dependency bump is a capability-affecting change that is Blair's to authorise, and — the
part that actually decided it — the nightly coder run has been live on this box since
23:04, and the gate and a coder run interfere. So the work is queued as #1455 and #1456
rather than half-done. The rejected alternative was to bump the pin behind a dormant flag
tonight and let him ratify it in the morning; I declined because "dormant" would still
have meant editing the tree during a live run, for a change with no deadline.

Two things I want the next session to inherit. First: **when we contribute upstream, the
downstream ticket that motivated the contribution needs a check-back predicate of its
own** — not just the upstream thread. We track the PR beautifully and the consequence not
at all. Second: the honest limit on all of this. I found #725 because I happened to search
the board for the crash signature while researching something else. There is no
instrument that would have surfaced it, and the deferral gate cannot see this class at
all — it checks that a predicate is *well-formed*, never that it has since come *true*.

**Lesson 362:** An upstream fix that lands does not update the downstream ticket
waiting for it. Merge-unblocks sweeps are written for our own merges, where an agent is
present at the moment of maximum knowledge; when the unblocking event happens in someone
else's repository, nobody is standing there, and a well-formed blocker predicate can stay
satisfied and unread indefinitely. Any ticket blocked on an external event needs a dated
check-back that is *its own*, not an assumption that whoever watches the upstream thread
will notice on its behalf.

**Next:** #1455 bumps the substrate to 2026.3.1 and satisfies #725's first clause; then
the 20/20 repro, then Blair's live tool turn re-arms a security control that has been
disarmed for eight weeks. #1456 tests whether an EAGLE-3 head beats our current draft
model on the short turns where #778 measured it losing.


### 2026-08-30 — The plan card promised what the coder was never told

*Plain summary: the operator asked whether he could give a dispatch a photo of himself; the answer was "partly", and the missing part turned out to be why his delivered websites keep containing an invented stranger.*

He asked a simple question tonight, in front of a plan waiting for his approval: could he hand it a photo? The answer looked like an easy yes. The `assets/` folder contract has been live since 2026-07-29, `advanced_intake_assets_pipeline = true`, and I could run `enumerate_operator_assets` against his project and watch it produce the plan-card line — *"Working from your assets folder: … the build is graded on actually using them."* I wrote him four content files, told him to drop in a photo, and very nearly told him he was done.

What stopped me was a habit rather than a suspicion: trace the corridor before promising it. The manifest is built AO-side by reading the filesystem. The coder runs somewhere else entirely, out in `agentic-setup`, from a git worktree. So I asked the question that the card's confident wording had made feel unnecessary — does the coder actually receive these files?

It receives them. `new-agent-task.ps1:115-121` commits any dirty tree as a baseline before cutting the worktree, so the files are physically there. That part was fine. What was not fine sat one layer up: `Add-AssetHint`, the only code that names existing assets into the coder's prompt, globbed five image extensions and nothing else, non-recursively. Every document, every video, every font the operator had ever placed in that folder was committed into the coder's tree and **never mentioned to it**. The card said the build was graded on using them. The coder was never told they existed.

And the grading was worse than silent. `_ensure_operator_assets_floor` injected a machine criterion asserting the provided filenames appear *in the built page markup* — a demand that, aimed at `bio.md`, asks for exactly the wrong outcome. A link to a biography is not a biography. The check would have been satisfied by an anchor tag pointing at the file, and the page could still have been written about a person who does not exist.

That is the mechanism behind a complaint he has made in one form or another since July: the sites come back with a fabricated stranger in them. I had been reading that as the coder's weakness. It was ours. Nothing was carrying his words into the build, and the one instrument that mentioned his files was checking for the wrong thing.

So the fix has three parts rather than the one he asked for. His actual request was a bigger size limit — *"i need the file limit size to be much larger than 25mb… like, 2Gb worth of assets"* — and asked, fairly, whether limits were needed at all. They are, though not for the reason a laptop suggests: because the fleet commits that folder into the project's git history before every build, and no destructive git operation exists here to take it back, an unbounded folder is an unbounded and unrecoverable repository, re-checked-out once per candidate. I raised the per-file cap from 25 MB to 2 GB and the total from 200 MB to 3 GB — deliberately not a round doubling of the file cap, because the disk cost is the total times the candidate count and he had just said he did not want his laptop filled. `.mov`, `.m4v`, `.mkv`, `.avi` joined the allowlist as a new `video` class; phone footage had been refused by type as well as by size.

The second part is that the plan card now discloses the total once it passes 64 MB, because approving that card is what commits the bytes, and the cost belongs where the decision is. The third is `Add-AssetHint` itself, now three blocks instead of one: images are referenced, video and audio are embedded, and documents are **read**. The document block says so explicitly and overrides `Add-WebHint`'s standing "do not spend turns reading the seed before you start" for those specific files, because reading the operator's own words is not exploratory reading. It also forbids the two failure shapes I could name from delivered sites — linking the document instead of writing its content out, and inventing a biography for something the file already answers.

The rejected design was to inject document *contents* into the coder's prompt. That is the obvious move and I did not take it: contents crossing that boundary is precisely what the manifest was built to avoid (`acceptance.py:3120-3121` — names, classes and sizes, never contents), and a file dropped in a folder is an instruction-shaped surface. Telling the coder to open a file it already has in its own tree keeps the trust boundary where it was and gets the same words onto the page.

Then live verification did the thing live verification does. Running the finished function against `C:\Users\mrbla\projects\blairproweb` instead of a fixture, every file came out **twice** — once as `assets/…`, once as `Assets/…`. The mapping table lists both spellings, and on a case-insensitive filesystem they are the same directory. That bug predates tonight by months. The suite could not see it because all fifteen of its assertions were `contains` checks, and a duplicate satisfies a `contains` check perfectly. AH34 and AH35 now count occurrences.

The suite went from 15 checks to 35, all passing, and one of the original fifteen had to be inverted. AH3 asserted that a `.txt` in the assets folder produces a byte-identical no-op — "never lists a .txt", marked `[kill]`. It was a correct rule when only images could mean anything, and it had become a lock holding the defect in place. I rewrote it rather than deleted it: a document is still never dressed as an image, which is the part that was always right. Python side, `shared/tests/test_operator_assets.py` went 43 → 46, and the one failure I caused was the test asserting a spreadsheet must appear in page markup — the same wrong demand, written down as an expectation.

Shipped live rather than dormant, and I want the reasoning visible because I told him the opposite at the gate: this is work inside a capability already ceremonied on 2026-07-29, with no new door, no new egress, no new surface, and the allowlist still closed. He directed the size raise himself. What changed is the bounds and the wording, not the posture.

One thing stays open and is not a deferral: `assets/reference/` still seeds a README telling him to put screenshots there for the build to emulate, and nothing consumes them — M6 is unbuilt. That instruction is in front of him on every new project. It is a one-clause honesty fix, recorded on #1165 and on #1469, and it is being made in this same arc rather than filed to feel finished.

The gate held at **11272 passed / 0 failed / 7 skipped / 133 deselected** (481.93 s), the seven skips all naming `AO loopback port 5001 is in use` — his app was open, which is the documented signature and the reason the pinned baseline moves to 11279 rather than the measured 11272. Fleet side, `verify-assets.ps1` went 15 checks to 37.

**Next:** he fills in the `TODO_BLAIR:` lines in the four content files, drops in a photo and whatever footage he wants, and dispatches `blairproweb` with the `advanced` token in the position that actually parses — first, right after `/dispatch`, which is the other thing tonight taught us.


### 2026-08-31 — The instrument improved when work was deleted, and so did two of the tests I wrote to catch it

*Plain summary: a probe scored a one-page website higher than a three-page one, because the only
check it had got easier the less you shipped. I fixed it, then wrote two regression tests that
passed against the very defect they were named for — and only found that out by breaking the fix
on purpose.*

The day began with a coder running at ~3 tokens per second against a banked 38.58 baseline, and
ended having retracted the baseline, the regression, and my own headline claim about the fix. What
survived is worth more than what I set out to do.

**The measurement that dissolved the problem.** Every throughput figure this project holds came
from `benchmark_ovms_http.py`, which generates 256-token completions. The coder sends ~7,000-token
prompts and writes page-sized files. Nobody had ever measured that. When I did — four consecutive
realistic generations per configuration, resources sampled continuously — the `cache_size` cliff I
had spent the afternoon explaining shrank from 1.9× to **11%**, and today's short probe at the
old setting measured 35.5 against June's 38.58. There may have been no regression at all in the
work the coder actually does. The instrument was the defect.

Then it got worse, in the useful direction. I reported the offloaded configuration as **46%
faster** off a four-run pass. An eight-run pass gave 11.82 tok/s. A third gave 9.59. Same
configuration, same idle box, one hour: **16.55 → 11.82 → 9.59**, and the session's first run was
16.82 while its last was 6.07. GPU memory stayed flat at 15.7 GiB the whole way down, and the CPU
was untouched — 400,000 hashes in 0.06 s. Twelve minutes idle and a full server restart made it
*worse*, not better.

**That is now #1487, and it outranks the work that found it.** The effect is ~40% across a session;
the difference between the two configurations I was comparing is ~12%. **Any A/B run sequentially
on this machine measures elapsed session time as much as the variable under test — the arm that
runs second loses.** Today's sweep ran sequentially, best arm first. So did the bisect that
produced the "13× cliff". Nothing in any performance stamp records *when in the session* a number
was taken, which is why it was invisible; it is the same class of unrecorded variable as the GPU
budget that #1485 exists for. Calling the variance "noise" earlier was wrong in a way worth
keeping: noise is symmetric, and this is monotonic.

The configuration change shipped anyway, and the reason it survives is that its justification was
never speed. Memory replicated to 0.6% across three passes — the old setting at 18.04 and 18.18 GiB
against a measured 17.98 GiB budget, over the line twice; the new one at 15.64 and 15.73, under it
twice. Being over budget is reproducible. Being faster is not. The coder now holds twice the
context it did this morning, and that is what removes the compaction risk hanging over the next
build, not any tokens per second.

**The instrument that rewarded deletion.** #1439's probe had been built on 17 August and never
merged, which is the only reason it kept ranking first on the roadmap. I did not discover that
until after I had rebuilt it from scratch, because I read the ticket's *description* and the
roadmap's row and never listed its *comments* — where two weeks of prior work, and a correction I
then re-asserted as a novel finding, were sitting. The roadmap warns about exactly this gap in its
own text: its checker catches a closed ticket but cannot catch an open one whose remaining work is
not what the row says.

The existing probe was better than my duplicate, so I abandoned mine and put the real one through
adversarial review. It found the thing that mattered. The check for *"the website should have a
home page, a pieces list page, individual piece pages, and a contact page"* was decided by a single
audit — *is every page I shipped linked from somewhere?* — and **a site gets better at that by
shipping fewer pages.** One delivery ships a single content page, has no contact page, and its menu
links to two files that do not exist. It scored SATISFIED, and it **outscored a strictly better
three-page build**. Eleven of twenty-one deliveries passed; nine of those shipped no piece page at
all. The cause was one sentence of prose that never matched its code: the function's own docstring
promised a page-count check nobody had written.

**And then I wrote two tests for it that could not fail.** The first compared the two deliveries'
total satisfied counts — but deleting pages also degrades three other criteria, so the total falls
while the defect improves, and the assertion held against the thing it named. The second compared
the criterion before and after deleting pages — but under the defect *both* read SATISFIED, so it
held too. Both were green. Both were decoration. I found out only by mutating the fix back out and
watching them stay green; the third version reproduces the real inversion — orphan a page so the
check fails, then delete the orphan, and the verdict *improves* — and it bites.

Two tests that cannot fail, written inside an hour, by the author of a fix, in a session whose
entire subject is instruments that report success on things a human would fail. That is the
argument for toggle-proving every lock, and it is not a hypothetical one any more.

**The strongest verification of the day came from someone who had not seen the fix.** The reviewer
computed in advance what a correct one must produce — the criterion passing on exactly two of
twenty-one deliveries, and named which two. Mine produces exactly those. It also caught me
mid-report: I had told the operator "0 of 21 now pass", measured before I had fixed a regex bug,
and 0 would have been the over-correction — an instrument that trades a false positive for
blindness. Without an independent target I would have shipped believing the check was stricter
than it is.

**Two smaller things that each cost a measurement.** OVMS folds `--cache_dir` into its plugin
config, so passing both is invalid and the server dies during load with a **zero-byte stderr** —
indistinguishable from a model failure. And PowerShell's `Start-Process -ArgumentList` strips
embedded double quotes, so a JSON argument arrives as `{OFFLOAD_RATIO:30}` and fails identically.
Each killed an experiment arm before I understood it. Separately, my shell ate backslashes out of
regexes **three times**, once producing an invisible 0x08 byte inside a pattern, and Windows line
endings silently rewrote three files — including one that *grew* by 281 bytes while I was trying to
shrink it by 121. All four are now field notes.

**A prediction missed by 25 and paid for itself.** I predicted the post-merge gate at 11,317; it
printed 11,342. Chasing rather than overwriting showed the difference is exactly the 2 failures and
23 skips a worktree produces and a real checkout does not — which means the *previous* baseline was
itself a worktree figure, understating the real tree by 25, with both baseline surfaces agreeing
with each other while both were wrong. That is the third occurrence of a failure `TEST_GOVERNANCE`
already warned about in the abstract.

**Who did what**

- **BlarAI did:** nothing today. No dispatch ran. Its coder is now under its memory budget with
  double the context, and an instrument that will grade its next delivery honestly — but it is
  still 0 for 5 on producing a page anyone has looked at.
- **Blair did:** asked the question that stopped the day's biggest mistake — *"it sounds like we
  are trying to force something that won't work; do we need to stop and work on offloading?"* — at
  the exact moment I was about to hand him a sixth dispatch onto a machine I had just measured as
  marginal. He was right and I had been carrying momentum. He also set the priority that reordered
  everything: the production environment before the website. And he closed his own app so the
  measurements would be clean.
- **Claude did:** shipped a config change on evidence that replicates, merged an instrument that
  had sat unmerged for two weeks, and found the machine-level defect that undermines the project's
  entire measurement history. Also published a 46% speed claim off four runs, rebuilt work that
  already existed because it read a description instead of a comment tail, re-asserted an error
  that had been retracted two weeks earlier, wrote two tests that could not fail, and edited the
  working tree while a gate was running. Four of those were caught by an independent reviewer or
  by the operator, not by me.

**Next:** reboot, then re-measure the same configuration. Recovery to ~16 tok/s means #1487 is
session-accumulated state and the answer is a scheduled restart before every campaign and before
the nightly; staying near 6 means it survives boots and the investigation moves to driver or
firmware. Those need opposite responses, which is why it is the first thing to do and not the last.

### 2026-08-31 (evening) — A ship that was finished two hours before the board knew, and a slowdown a reboot simply deleted

*Plain summary: the machine had been getting steadily slower all day on one unchanged setting; it
was restarted and the slowness was gone, which tells us to restart before long runs rather than
chase a hardware fault. And the day's biggest piece of work had been complete since half past five
while every surface a later session reads still said it was unbuilt.*

I picked up a handoff card whose next step was a single perishable measurement, and the machine had
rebooted ninety seconds before I read the clock. That is the whole reason tonight decided anything.

**The measurement.** #1487 had recorded the identical coder configuration at 16.55, then 11.82,
then 9.59 tokens per second inside one hour on an idle box. Twelve minutes of idle did not recover
it; a full server restart made it worse. Nobody knew whether a reboot would, and the two answers
demanded opposite work — a restart discipline, or a driver-and-firmware investigation. On the
freshly booted machine the same configuration gave first run **16.55** against pass 1's 16.50,
median **16.05** against 16.55, and within-pass drift **-6.3%** against -6.7%.

That first-run figure is a correction. I originally wrote pass 1's as 16.82, and 16.82 belongs to
`cs2-nooffload-CONTROL` — a different configuration, measured ten minutes later. Pass 1's own first
run is 16.50, so the boot-fresh run was marginally *faster*: the delta changes sign and the
conclusion strengthens. Independent review caught it before the merge, and I had propagated it to
five surfaces including a ticket description. What I had done was borrow a **session-wide** maximum
into a **config-specific** row — same units, same "first run" framing, a value two percent away,
the wrong record, and nothing in the citation to show it. I minted a lesson about instruments
crediting evidence they never checked the provenance of, four hours before committing this.

I judged it on those three figures rather than the median alone, and that was the operator's
correction, not my instinct. My plan had said "roughly 16 means session state, roughly 6 means it
survives boots", and he pointed out that #1487 itself records **-37.3% drift inside one four-run
pass** — so a median of four can land near 12 from drift alone and decide nothing by construction.
A decision rule that can be satisfied by the noise it is meant to see through is not a decision
rule. With three figures compared like-for-like there is no ambiguity: all three land on pass 1.

So the decay is state accumulated inside an operating-system session, and a restart is the entire
remedy. What I was careful not to write is the sentence one step further: this does **not** show
the box can exceed ~16.8 tok/s, because pass 1 was itself taken in a session already doing GPU
work. A reboot returns to pass 1's state; pass 1 was never proven to be the ceiling. And the
within-pass drift *reproduced* — -6.7% then -6.3% — so the reboot resets the accumulator without
removing it, and a long campaign still ends slower than it starts. Rebuilding the nightly around
"we reboot first, therefore throughput is flat" would be a design built on the half of the finding
I like.

The rejected alternative is worth naming. The obvious structural control is a guard that refuses to
measure on a machine that has been up too long, in the shape of the AO-resident guard shipped this
morning. I did not build it, because I cannot support a threshold: what predicts decay is
accumulated GPU **work** in the session, which nothing measures, and uptime would be wrong for a
box booted eight hours ago and idle. An inferred constant published as a control is precisely what
#1477 had to retract earlier today. So the stamp records run-index, elapsed session time and machine
age (`cf0cceb6`), the console prints the machine's age beside the drift line labelled as a proxy,
and the battery-side response — reboot before every campaign — is #1490 with a predicate rather
than a ritual I could not distinguish from a working control.

**The other half of the night was bookkeeping that turned out not to be bookkeeping.** #1439's
probe merged at 17:30 as `d483ffef`. Its disposition record merged twenty minutes later as
`c3511b5f`, eight findings, all FIXED, verifier PASS. And the ticket was still open, its newest
comment still ending *"No disposition record exists … that is the next action here"*, #1321 —
the ticket that depends on its output — carrying nothing newer than 17 August, and the roadmap's
rank 1 still instructing the next session to **build** an instrument that was already on `main`.

Every piece of that ship existed. Nothing had been told. The disposition is filed as
`docs/reviews/delivery-criteria-report-disposition-2026-08-31.md` — **the filename carries no
ticket number**, so the one search a later reader runs, grep `1439` in `docs/reviews/`, returns
nothing and the blocker reads as outstanding. That is not a clerical detail: earlier the same day a
session rebuilt this very probe from scratch because it read the ticket's description and the
roadmap row and never listed its comments. Two rebuilds-from-finished-work in one day, from the
same root, which is that the artifact and the record of the artifact live in different places and
only one of them is read.

The roadmap row was also, separately, one day from turning the standing gate red. `_MAX_LAG_DAYS`
is 14 and its refresh stamp had reached exactly 14 days behind `main`; one commit dated 1 September
would have failed `test_the_roadmap_is_not_stale`. I removed the finished row and renumbered 1..21
rather than re-describing it, because `verify_roadmap.py` flags a closed cited ticket as STALE and
the form test needs ranks contiguous from one — the file's own #1005 precedent. Nothing was
re-ranked; retiring a row for demonstrably shipped work is not a judgement about what matters next,
and that judgement is not mine. The verifier went from `1 stale of 22` to `PASS, 21 rows, all
cited tickets open`, which is the check earning its keep twice: it named the row to remove and
certified that no other row points at finished work.

**Who did what.** The operator verified my gate independently before confirming it and returned
five corrections, of which two changed outcomes rather than wording: the decision rule above, and
the instruction to close #1439 citing the disposition as well as the merge — without which the
ticket's own last-stated blocker would have gone unanswered by the comment that closed it. He also
told me to let tonight's nightly fire rather than hold the night clear, which was the right call
and the opposite of my recommendation; the measurement I was protecting the night for took four
minutes. I banked a decisive measurement, closed a ship that had been complete and silent, folded
four journal fragments, and opened three tickets. I also nearly wrote a decision rule that could
not decide.

Merged this arc: `cf0cceb6`, `027262a8`, `0c0d9b40`. Gate on the branch and re-run on merged main
below. Lessons 357-360 come from the folded fragments; 361 from tonight. #1439 closed, #1487
decided on its central question and still open on its mechanism, #1488 the arc, #1489 and #1490
opened.

**Next:** tonight's 23:00 nightly is armed and fires unattended on a machine that has now been up
since 19:07 — the first battery night this project can read against a known machine age, and the
first test of whether #1490 is worth building in the shape #1487 predicts.

### 2026-08-31 (late evening) — The window we doubled was never open, and the gate that found it was red for a file in another repository

*Plain summary: this afternoon's change to give the coder twice as much room to read was written
into a file that only records what the system does, not one that tells it what to do. The coder
has been running at half the intended size all evening. It is fixed and all four places now
agree.*

I re-ran the standing test suite before merging tonight's work and it came back **11,344 passed,
3 failed**. Two failures were mine and cosmetic. The third was not mine at all:

```
coder-30b: limit.context opencode=32768 vs profile.effective_context=65536
```

That test, `test_model_profiles_ssot.py`, exists to catch one setting drifting between the files
that mirror it. It was doing its job about a change made four hours earlier in a different
repository.

**What had happened.** `7762e50` doubled the coder's context window from 32,768 to 65,536 and
edited `agentic-setup/configs/opencode.json` to say so. That file is a **mirror, not a source**.
`sync-harness.ps1` runs one way — it copies the *live* `~/.config/opencode/opencode.json` over the
repo's copy whenever the two differ, and auto-commits. Nobody had deployed to the live file. So
the repo edit sat there being wrong until the next sync overwrote it and committed the
overwrite: `b8f925c`, *"harness sync: live config drift captured"*, at **19:39:45** — six seconds
before an OVMS process started.

That OVMS start was mine. My own `start-llm.ps1` invocation triggered the revert, and the console
printed *"Harness change detected — recorded in setup history: configs/opencode.json"* while I
read it as routine noise on my way to a measurement. **The system told me, correctly, at the exact
moment, and I did not hear it.**

**The trap is documented, and it names its own prior victim.** `verify-config-deploy-sync.ps1`
opens with it: *"sync-harness.ps1 is one-way LIVE→repo … So a repo-only fix that is not ALSO
deployed live gets reverted at the next sync (proven in the wild: `f219074`'s bash:deny reverted
3h later by `3553b56`)."* There is a guard. It is careful, toggle-proved across seven scenarios,
and it checks **one** of the five destinations the sync writes — `configs/agents/review.md`. Not
`configs/opencode.json`. A real control, correct in everything it covers, covering a fifth of the
surface, and the escaping four-fifths cost a live capability for an evening. That is #1491.

There is a second, quieter error in the record. `f29c934`'s commit message reads *"the context
window is a THREE-file setting, not two"*, and lists `model-profiles.json`, `configs/opencode.json`
and `start-llm.ps1`. **It is four**, and the fourth is the only one that governs: the live file
outside the repository. Three of the four were correct the whole time, which is exactly why
nothing looked wrong. Counting the surfaces is not the same as finding the one with authority.

**The fix, and one thing I got wrong inside it.** Set the live file, then copy live → mirror so the
two are byte-identical and the next sync is a no-op — disarmed rather than dodged. My first attempt
edited the live JSON by loading and re-dumping it, which re-escaped an em dash in a model name into
`\u2014` and rewrote the formatting of a file I had no business reformatting. I restored the
original bytes and did it surgically instead: one number, file length unchanged, every other byte
identical. A config belonging to another program is not mine to normalise, and I would not have
noticed if I had not diffed it.

Verified after the change, all four read 65,536: the live `~/.config/opencode/opencode.json`, the
mirror, `model-profiles.json`'s `effective_context`, and `openclaw.json5`'s `contextWindow`.
`qwen3-14b` at 32,768 and the vision model at 16,384 deliberately untouched. And it fits the
server: `cache_size 4` holds 87,381 tokens, the declared window is 65,536 + 8,192 = 73,728, so the
client compacts at 84% before the server can evict — a margin that matters because OVMS does not
refuse an over-large request, it dies with an empty error log. The gate that found it: 10 passed.

The trade-off I took deliberately: I changed the coder's live behaviour roughly two and a half
hours before an unattended battery run, having told the operator earlier that changing the shipped
configuration was out of scope tonight. I judged the alternative worse — a night spent building at
half the intended context while every record, and the operator, believed otherwise. It is his
priority that made the call easy: he had said the working development environment matters more
than the website. But tonight's run is now a **new arm**, not a continuation, and any comparison
against last night's has that in it.

Shipped: `agentic-setup` `32cb5dc`. Opened: #1491. Banked at #1477 (comment 3313).

**Next:** tonight's 23:00 run is the first with the window the config work was meant to deliver —
and, if the operator reboots beforehand, the first ever to start on a machine of known age.


### 2026-08-31 — Monthly retrospective: the month we stopped reading controls and started counting what they did

*Plain summary: August turned the instrument-honesty problem from July into a method. Nearly every finding came from dividing something (events by outcomes, runs by artifacts, claims by evidence) rather than from reading code or a status line, and my own corrections, including several to claims I made this month, came the same way.*

August was not the repair month that July's bill implied, and the journal says why in its own first week: the repairs kept uncovering a second layer. The month holds 61 entries (08-05 x1 plus the July retrospective filed that day, 08-06 x9, 08-07 x7, 08-08 x8, 08-09 x7, 08-10 x3, 08-11 x1, 08-14 x6, 08-15 x9, 08-16 x3, 08-17 x2, then nothing from 08-18 to 08-28, then 08-29 x1, 08-30 x1, 08-31 x3). It divides into three pieces. Thirty-six entries fall between 08-05 and 08-11 and are one long argument about the coder's website job, B9 (the multi-page pottery site), and whether anything that graded it could fail. Twenty fall between 08-14 and 08-17, after four days away, and are the Definition of Done (DoD) rows being audited against disk. Five come after the gap, on a machine that had stopped producing the website at all.

The first piece starts with a word the system could not hear. On 08-05 the operator asked for a "page" and was told a command-line tool was being built, because `operator_platform_signal` carried "web page" and "website" and not bare "page". Nothing raised an error. It was the third instance that day, so the fix was a table, `OPERATOR_WEB_VOCABULARY`, that generates its own tests (17 new locks; gate 10162 / 0 / 0 on the merged tip). Widening it broke the system's own prose: a planner-facing steer that says "names no platform" began reading as the operator naming one, and that test went red in the same run as the new ones.

The B9 run then taught the month's central lesson in a single sequence, 08-07 to 08-10. Five attempts failed before the first built anything, and two of them printed the identical message, "No response from the Assistant Orchestrator", for unrelated causes. The first task took 39m43s and the next two took 8m08s and 10m58s, so banking the first sample would have reversed the conclusion. On 08-08 I opened an archived site instead of reading its scorecard and found that the highest-authority exam in the system, 33 lines and five tests of which two had empty bodies, passed on a tree with no product, and that an optimisation (#1049) had used that pass to delete the individual-piece page from the plan, two nights running. The root was four lines: the fail-to-pass baseline returned early for any oracle whose path did not end in `.py`. Reading every `oracle-qa.json` ever written then showed the baseline had never produced a result: 81 of 81 read `not-run`, so closing the node skip (#1342) did not make it start. I had told the operator a root cause when I had found a root cause. The same day he found three defects in a minute by clicking links that four gates had passed, and I built the cheapest instrument available, a link checker, which on its first full sweep reported "clean" for four empty scaffolds. It now reports `checked: false` when it scanned nothing.

The same day produced two corrections to myself that I want kept in place. I announced 54 wasted generated images across the corpus, priced at 54 minutes of GPU; 29 of the 54 were his own family photographs in a folder the scaffold told him to use, and the real figure was 11. And the 25 percent speedup I attributed to one corrected sentence in the coder's instructions (home-page 67.3 min with 11 permission denials, pieces-list 50.2 min with 0) is the denials count only: the minutes are n=1 per arm and confounded, and the entry says so.

On 08-09 the lost builds turned out to be a deadline, not a fault, and it took seven wrong hypotheses to see it. The fix worked and my explanation of it was wrong; the entry that followed retracts it. I had written that eight assets at 59.9 s each (479 s) overran a 180 s handshake budget. The handshake had in fact taken 16.35 s to negotiate against a 5 s per-attempt ceiling, nine failures into the log before any image began, and dispatch generation measured 37.9 s per asset (303 s for eight), not 59.9. Two of the four constants I had changed earned their place; two were unproven headroom and now say so. The registry line I had written, "SHRINK back to ~5 s once generation moves off the request thread", was an instruction to re-break the system. The same afternoon I filed a ticket telling a future session to weaken a stall detector that was working; the coder had started `node src/server.js` and waited 602 s for a server to exit, and a search of 932 agent logs found 17 server-starting commands. A correction dated 08-10 06:20 then withdrew my claim that the coder runtime had no per-command timeout: a plugin had capped bash at 300 s with a 10 s server probe for a month. It had fired (`"timeout": 10000`) and could not have helped, because a trailing `&` made the shell exit first and the tool then read a pipe the detached server held open. A second correction that morning withdrew the first: the 3,221-byte home page I had reported was never delivered; `public/index.html` shipped at 1,044 bytes and the repaired work was discarded when the fix pass timed out (#1370). Three readings of one dispatch, each corrected by the next measurement.

The counting method named itself on 08-10. Five controls had never changed an outcome, and each was found by dividing: 35 visual-fix cycles, 11 applied, 24 discarded, all 24 on the same branch; 269 permission `ask` events raised, none answered, 256 of 256 gated commands completed; the coverage map skipped on every run because "too thin" counted as a hard finding. I changed a recommendation on a percentage rather than an argument: interpreters (`python -m`, `python -c`, `node -e`) were 54 percent of 4,193 commands, so an allowlist cannot bound the coder.

The second piece, 08-14 to 08-17, applied the same method to the DoD itself. The battery had banked nothing since 08-10 because a guard that counts TASK-START against TASK-END was wedged by a Windows Update restart at 03:29 on 08-12, and its escape hatch asked the dead run to sign its own death certificate; the fix was a boot-time predicate, and the ticket specifying it had been filed three days earlier and cleared by hand, which cost five nights. I measured the machine's boots over four days and wrote that into the code as a property of the machine; over fourteen days there were nine hibernate resumes, and two reviews said so. A sweep of 63 verifiers found five red on main, and the explanations differed (one genuine, two alarms nobody had retired, two caused by how I ran them). Four DoD rows asserted a thing had never happened or could not be measured and the measurement was already banked. Capability 13's audit of fifteen graders found six mechanisms by which a published figure reads cleaner than the truth, and the sixth was in a fix I had written an hour earlier (`status` carried the caveat, `summary()` shipped to the scorecard did not).

The 08-15 merges were `739d2163` (coverage_partial across every bounded grader, gate 11057 / 0 / 0) and `a691bd77`. Independent review then reopened three of ten closed tickets, and the gate test I wrote to catch the class (`b6812ebd`) passed with the flag deleted because its threshold was met by my own comment. The 08-15 interview fix (#1406) took four review rounds, each locally right and one layer short. On 08-16 the coder-test grader, which read only Python, was found to have reported "no tests" for 55 of 55 website records; the node reader needed fourteen further fixes in two families (eight about where a declaration's body begins, six about declarations it did not recognise). The 08-16 entry splits that count twice, once in a draft I miscounted. The goal "every DoD row LIVE" stood at 3 of 21 on 08-15 at 15:26 and again that evening; four of the fifteen graders cannot examine a website, giving a ceiling of 11 of 15 that rose to 12. On 08-17 the retraction that mattered was a lock I had written that required a retracted premise (`e2e31146`, lesson 358); a delivery-criteria probe scored three banked deliveries 3/2/3, 3/3/2 and 5/1/2, and its own tally across twelve was first recorded 6/5/1 and re-measured by review as 7/4/1.

The last piece, after the gap, is quieter and more expensive. On 08-29 the upstream fix for the grammar-constrained crash behind #725 (PR #4082, merged 8 July, shipped in OpenVINO GenAI 2026.3.0.0 on 5 August) had been unread by its own ticket for twenty-four days, while the control stays disarmed (`tool_call_grammar = false`) pending #1455 and a live turn. On 08-30 the assets folder was found to reach the coder's tree but never its prompt, which is the likely mechanism behind the invented stranger in delivered sites. On 08-31 the throughput work dissolved: the 256-token benchmark that every figure came from does not resemble 7,000-token coder prompts, the 1.9x cache cliff shrank to 11 percent, and one unchanged configuration read 16.55, 11.82, 9.59 tok/s in an hour (#1487). I had published 46 percent faster off four runs. A reboot returned the same configuration to 16.55 against pass 1's 16.50; that shows the decay is session state, not that 16.8 is a ceiling. The context doubling that afternoon had been written into a mirror that `sync-harness.ps1` overwrites from the live file, so the coder ran at 32,768 all evening until a red test in another repository caught it (#1491).

What August did not resolve. B9 has still never delivered a site that was reviewed and called good: it graded GREEN in 1 of 18 attempts on the byte-identical goal (08-15; the 08-31 entry says it is still 0 for 5 on producing a page anyone has looked at, a different window). The Python nightly rows stay unmeasured by his standing decision (#1385). `green_audit`, `oracle_mutation` and the ruff pass cannot read a website. The forms clause measures coverage and not verdict (#1434). The public mirror, which July called frozen, was cleared (DEC-12) and republished on 08-09, a retraction of that July sentence; the first publish had to be rerun after I reported the bookkeeping realigned and he asked why the repos had not changed.

**Next:** the reboot-before-campaign decision (#1490) and the mechanism behind #1487; #1455 and the 20/20 repro that would re-arm the grammar control; #1491's guard, which checks one of five sync destinations; and the website job's first night on a coder holding the window it was configured to have.
